Only the client's own people get a login: a contact whose relationship is `employee` or `contractor`, or whose organization has kind `own`, and who has an email (core's `canInviteContact`, with `isOwnPerson`; vendors, customers, partners and others never do). // DECISION: new routes, one per surface, rather than a `contactId` on the invite routes: `POST /api/contacts/:id/invite` (client admin) and `POST /api/ops/clients/:id/contacts/:contactId/invite` (operator, served clients, not the Sandbox), body `{role, canSign?}` only (strict: an `email` or `name` is refused, 400). They read the contact in the client's scope (another client's is 404), refuse a person who isn't the client's own (422 `not_invitable`) or has no email (422 `no_email`), then run the same `inviteToClient` with the contact's email and name: the same roles, refusals, email after the commit and audit row. Status with no migration: the directory list (client admin and operator only; client users don't get it) gives each person `access`: `member` when a member of the client has their email (lower case), `invited` when that member hasn't signed in yet, else null. // DECISION: an invite is a membership, so `invited` is a member whose sign-in says they haven't signed in; with the dev sign-in, which can't tell, they read as a member, as on the Team page. The contact's organization now carries its `kinds`, so the screens apply the same rule. Screens: "Invite to sign in" on an invitable person who can't sign in yet (the back office's Directory tab, the portal's Contacts details for the client admin) opens a dialog with the name and email read-only, a role (client user by default) and "Can approve" (on; always on for an admin); the result in `inviteMessage`'s words; badges "Can sign in" and "Invited, hasn't signed in yet"; an own person with no email shows "Add an email to invite". The Team page and the client's settings link to the directory next to the email form, which stays. Portal strings in six languages. Open: a fluent speaker's review of the new strings. |

### 1.15 Decisions made in stream M11 (usage and reports)

ARCHITECTURE §13–§15: usage and cost for the back office, the portal's activity report and service page. Notification emails and the M11 Playwright e2e are not part of this stream (they need email and the Pia pack).

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| When usage rolls up | **On write, plus an hourly sweep.** When a run finishes, `rollupUsageForRun` recomputes every UTC day from the run's creation to now (at most 31): a run that waited overnight adds steps and actions to later days. The cron, at minute 7 of each hour (`USAGE_SWEEP_MINUTE`), recomputes today and yesterday for every client, so a run still waiting shows its cost within the hour. No GET writes. The rollup is a recompute from the rows, so running it twice gives the same answer. No migration (0009 unused). |
| What usage counts | Per client agent and UTC day (as before): runs created, step runs (by when they started), their tokens and cost, external actions executed (`done`), and, new, `documents`: distinct documents the agent's versions extracted that day (`doc_extraction.agent_version_id`). Background reads (no agent) are no agent's usage. Tool calls have no column of their own: their cost is their step's, and the writes among them are `external_actions`. Test runs count: their model calls cost the operator just the same. |
| Money | Cost is summed by Postgres as numeric (`sum(cost_usd)::text`), then across months and rows with decimal.js (`Decimal` from core); counts come back as text too. The wire carries 4-place strings; the screen shows 2 places, or 4 under a cent ("$0.0003"), so small runs don't read as free. |
| `GET /api/ops/usage` | `?client=&agent=&template=&from=&to=` (days, inclusive, UTC). Default: this month and the five before. At most 731 days; `from` after `to` or a longer range answers 422. Answers the months in range, the total and each month, and rows per client, client agent (with its template, or none for an agent built for one client; test copies flagged) and template (across its forks and test copies, with how many clients and agents), each with every month. Scoped to the clients the operator serves (`?client=` of one not served answers 403, as every ops route); the query filters by `tenant_id` too. |
| Portal figures | `GET /api/reports/activity?from=&to=&service=` and `GET /api/services/:id` count from the rows, not from `usage_daily`: the rollup is the operator's ledger (UTC days, test runs included), a client's report is its own days (`tenant.timezone`) and live work only. Runs by how they ended (done, failed, cancelled, waiting, going), documents received (uploaded or forwarded) and read (extracted; a test run's read still counts, the document was read), changes made to its tools by connector. Never cost, tokens or prompts: the contract has no such fields (the contract test checks every portal route). Default period: this month so far in the client's time zone. |
| Which agents are services | Client agents that have gone live: active, paused or retired (a retired service keeps its page, so old links open); never drafts, agents in testing or test copies. Reports list the active and paused ones, and any other that worked in the period. |
| Service page | `/services/:id` shows the service's name, description and status, this month's runs, documents read and changes, its last ten live runs (to `/runs/:id`), and its reports: Activity (`/reports?service=<id>`) and the pack reports of the client's packs. `GET /api/services/:id` is added beside ARCHITECTURE §14's routes for it; `GET /api/services` (the list) is still the overview's. |
| Pack reports | A slot, not built: `apps/web/src/packs/registry.ts` holds `PACK_REPORTS` (pack, slug, title, description, component), empty now. `/reports/<pack>/<report>` mounts a registered report when the client has its pack (the activity report names the client's packs from `tenant_pack`); otherwise it says the report isn't available. The Reports screen shows Activity, and a row of links once any pack report applies. |
| Back-office screen | `/ops/usage` (sidebar: Usage): stats for the range (cost, with this month's; runs; documents read and changes; tokens), a table by month, and a cost-by-month table grouped by client, client agent or template; a template row opens its client agents. Filters (`view`, `range`, `client`, `template`) live in the URL so other pages can link to a client's or template's usage. Ranges are whole months back from today in UTC (this month, 3, 6, 12). |

### 1.16 Track B's open items (record pages, activation polling, template test runs on a client, the back office's documents)

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Record pages | `GET /api/records/:id` (client roles, their own client) and `GET /api/ops/clients/:id/records/:recordId` (operator, one served client) answer what a source id (invariant 10) names: a `transaction` (kind, vendor, date, total, payment, purchaser, memo, status, lines, linked documents, the connector and its id there, the raw record it was made from), a `raw_record` (connector, entity type, its id and version there, when it arrived, the payload as it arrived, the transactions made from it), a `document` (an extraction id names its document) or a `vendor` (name, aliases, its 20 most recent transactions). Every query names the client besides RLS. Anything else (a pack's rows, runs, inbox items) answers 404 and the page says there is no record page for it: the platform never special-cases a pack. The contract is `packages/core/src/api/records.ts`; the routes are `apps/api/src/routes/records.ts`. |
| What the portal sees | The client's own records only, the same view as the back office's: no model, cost, agent version or `ext` fields. A raw record's payload is the client's own data from its own tool or file (a CSV row's cells, a webhook delivery's body), so it is shown as it arrived. |
| Record screens | `/records/:id` (portal) and `/ops/clients/:clientId/records/:recordId`; a document id goes to the document page. Sources show as Receipts (`mz-receipt`, "Record ·3f9a1c", the id's tail) linking to these pages on the portal timeline, the back-office run view (from each step's `output.sources`) and inbox items (their `payload.sources`, now Receipts rather than a fact). Ids that are not uuids stay plain receipts. A document page links the purchase it is matched to. |
| Activation and a running test run | `GET /api/ops/agents/:id/activation` also answers `testRun`: the live version's latest test run (`id`, `status`, `createdAt`), or null. While it is queued, running or waiting, the Settings tab says "A test run of vN is going" with a link and polls the check every 2 s (the run page's rate, §1.2 "Live updates"), so it turns ready when the run passes; a failed last run is said with its link. Starting a test run from the panel checks again at once. |
| Template test runs on a client | `POST /api/ops/agents/:id/versions/:vid/test-run` takes `clientId` (ARCHITECTURE §10.4): for a template, one of the clients the operator serves (404 otherwise); without it, the Sandbox as before. The template runs as its test copy in that client (§1.5, one per template and client), so the run, its steps, outputs, proposed actions and inbox items all belong to that client (invariant 1); it is a test run, so it never writes (invariant 8), its person steps go to the operator and it is hidden from the client's portal. The copy is never activated or routed, and is not a use of the template. A client agent test-runs on its own client only (422 for another `clientId`). The builder's test-run panel offers the Sandbox first, then each served client (`ClientSummary.sandbox` marks the Sandbox). |
| Test copy bindings | On each test run, a test copy's required connectors that are not bound yet are bound to its client's connection for that connector (a client has at most one per connector), logged as an operational change. Bindings the operator set are kept. This also closes the Track B integration note that a test copy had to be bound by hand before its actions ran. |
| The back office's documents | `GET /api/ops/clients/:id/documents` (newest first, 50 by default, 200 at most) and `GET /api/ops/clients/:id/documents/:documentId` answer the portal's document views (`DocumentSummary`, `DocumentDetail`, moved to `packages/core/src/api/documents.ts`) for one served client, with a fresh 5-minute link each time (the documents routes' signer). `/ops/clients/:clientId/documents` lists them (document, vendor, total, status, matched purchase, received) and a Documents link sits in the sidebar while a client is selected; the document page is the portal's view (`apps/web/src/documents/DocumentDetail.tsx`, shared) with back-office words. |
| The back office's records | `GET /api/ops/clients/:id/records` (`?kind=transactions\|vendors\|items`, `?q=`, `?source=` a connection id, `?offset=`, `?limit=` 50 by default, 200 at most) lists what a served client's connections brought in, with each kind's count (for the source asked, not the search), the latest sync and the sources. Transactions come from `transaction` (vendor, doc number from `ext.<connector>.docNumber`, memo, total and currency, counts of `transaction_line` and linked documents, deleted at source from `ext.<connector>.deleted`), their source and synced time from the raw record they were made from; newest first is by transaction date, then by arrival. Vendors are `vendor` rows, their source the raw `Vendor` record their external ref names (a vendor made from an upload has none and shows only under all sources). Items are QuickBooks' own: the latest raw version of each `Item` per connection (another connector's `Item` is not a product). The search matches doc number, memo or vendor (transactions), name or another name (vendors), name, SKU or description (items), case-insensitively, plus an amount (`$1,234.50`) against a total or an item's cost; no index (lists are per client). Sources are the client's connections that brought one of these in, plus any that have synced. `/ops/clients/:clientId/records` shows it (counts line, kind tabs, search, source filter, pages of 50; each row opens the record page) and a Records link sits under Documents in the sidebar. A connection card in the back office names a connection by `config.accountName` (the company, as the portal does) with the account id under it, and links "See what it synced" to its records once it has synced. The portal has no list. |
| Test-run payloads | A test run's input can be one of the client's recent events (ARCHITECTURE §10.4 "real data"; was open in §3's workflow builder row). `GET /api/ops/clients/:id/events?type=` (operator, one served client, 404 otherwise; `limit` 10 by default, 50 at most) answers that client's latest events of the type from the senses outbox (`event`), newest first, each with its payload as emitted and a plain label: a document event names the document's file name, `document.extracted` adds the extraction's vendor and total, a transaction event its date, vendor and total, anything else its payload's plain fields. It runs with withTenant's scope inside the request's transaction (`narrowToTenant` in packages/db, since the request holds the one pooled connection) and names the client in every query. Never a test run's: test runs emit nothing (`emit` with `testRun`), and an event whose payload names a test run (`runId`) is left out all the same. The Settings tab's test run and the builder's test-run panel offer the trigger's sample (when its type has one), "Use a recent event" (event triggers only; a template's from the Sandbox, or from the client chosen) and manual input; choosing one fills the JSON box, and editing it makes it manual. |
| The Sandbox's uploads | `POST /api/ops/clients/:id/uploads` is the portal's upload (`POST /api/uploads`, the same multipart, code and results, through the client's upload connection, recorded as uploaded by the operator) for the Sandbox client only, which has no client people: 403 `not_sandbox` in any other client, 404 for a client not served. `GET /api/ops/clients/:id/documents` answers `sandbox`, and the Sandbox's Documents page shows the portal's drop zone above the list. A byte-identical file again is the same document (one per client and sha256): `created` false, no event, not read again. |
| Migrations | None (0010 unused). |


### 1.17 Owner answers, 2026-11-04 (approver rules and billing, to build)

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Approver rules | Per client. The operator sets them; the client admin can only make them stricter (a stricter approver, never a looser one). A rule matches on any of: connector or tool, verb (`create`, `update`, …), an amount over a threshold, the client agent; it names the approver: the client admin, a named client user, a client role, or the operator on the client's behalf. Rules are ordered: the first match wins; with no match, the client admin approves. They decide *who* approves; whether a step needs approval at all stays per step and per matrix (invariant 8). A run snapshots the approver it used. |
| Billing | Stripe. Each client is a Stripe customer. A monthly **base fee per active client agent** (not per user), which includes an allowance of **credits**; usage beyond the allowance is billed per credit (Stripe metered prices with the allowance as the free tier), counted from `usage_daily`. Keys are network secrets, never in the repo. **One credit is one cent of what the work cost us** (owner, 2026-11-04): model cost plus provider costs (SMS and WhatsApp messages, call minutes), converted from `usage_daily` with unit costs kept as operator settings, so every new tool is billable without a new rule; the markup is in the overage price. The allowance and the prices are open (§7). |
| Millwork and Pia | Built separately. M8 is out of this plan's MVP path; its branches from the overnight wave are kept but not merged. **Superseded (v0.15, 2026-11-07):** the Materials pack is ported onto main as the Purchasing area (§1.63, M34). |


### 1.18 Languages (owner, 2026-11-04; stream I)

The portal and what agents write for clients come in the client's language. The back office stays in English.

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Languages | English (the source), Spanish, French, Portuguese (Brazil), Italian, German: `en`, `es`, `fr`, `pt-BR`, `it`, `de`. Regional formats (es-MX, fr-CA, pt-PT…) come from `Intl` with the reader's region; translations are shared per language. Adding a language is a new message file and a review, nothing else. Right-to-left languages are out. |
| Who picks | Each client has a default language (`tenant.settings.language`, set by the operator or the client admin); each user can choose their own, which wins. Sign-in and anything before a session follow the browser. |
| Portal text | Every portal and shared-component string goes through `t()` with a message file per language; dates, numbers and money through `Intl` in the reader's locale; API errors shown by their code in the reader's language. The back office keeps literal English. |
| Agents' client-facing text | Skill and tool summary templates may carry a version per language (falling back to English); the client's language is passed to the runtime, and model-written text that reaches the client is asked for in it. Step labels on the client timeline stay operator-written per client agent, in that client's language. |
| Translations | Written with the code, marked for review by a fluent speaker before a client sees them. Documents and invoices are not translated. |
| Next step (planned, not in stream I) | **Message translation.** Agents read and write in several languages and translate incoming text: a client can keep everything in one working language (e.g. Spanish emails, webhook payloads and thread messages in, English stored beside them) or the reverse for outgoing messages, per client and per contact. The original is always kept (raw records stay immutable, invariant 3); the translation is a tool result with `agent_version_id`, never instructions (invariant 9), and is stored beside the original. Built as P-7: §1.69. |

**Built in stream I (2026-11-04).** Decisions made while building:

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Where languages live | `packages/core/src/i18n/locale.ts`: the six `LOCALES`, `Locale` (Zod), `resolveLocale` (user, then client, then browser, then English), `matchLocale` (`es-MX` → `es`; every Portuguese tag → `pt-BR`), `parseAcceptLanguage`. Stored values are parsed, never trusted (invariant 5). |
| The client's default | `tenant.settings.language`. The operator sets it on the back office's client page (`PUT /api/ops/clients/:id/language`), the client admin from the portal's user menu (`PUT /api/client/language`). Once set it is changed, not cleared; unset, each reader's browser decides. |
| A user's own language | `tenant.settings.user_languages[<app_user id>]`, set by the user from the user menu (`PUT /api/me/language`, `null` follows the client again). **No migration** (0011 stays unused): a client user belongs to exactly one client and reads only its portal, and operators always read English. Keys are written with `jsonb_set`, so other settings are never overwritten. |
| The session | `GET /api/session` carries `language: { effective, user, client }`. The API resolves `effective` with `Accept-Language` as the browser. Operator sessions are always `en`. Before a session (sign-in, its errors) the app follows `navigator.languages`, else English. |
| The catalog | Our own, no library: `apps/web/src/i18n/messages/<locale>.ts`, English the source. Keys and `{placeholders}` are typed from `en.ts`, so a missing or extra key, or a missing parameter, fails the typecheck; `i18n.test.tsx` also checks every file has English's keys, plural shapes and placeholders. Plurals pick their form with `Intl.PluralRules` (`other` required). Numbers in parameters use the reader's grouping. All six files ship in the bundle. |
| Formats | `Intl` in the reader's region: the browser's own tag when it is the same language (`es-MX`, `pt-PT`, `fr-CA`), else the language; English without a region formats as `en-US`, as before. Money is formatted by `Intl.NumberFormat` straight from its decimal string (exact, ECMA-402 NumberFormat v3), never through a float (invariant 6). Unit prices keep up to 4 places. Durations are message strings (`{n} min`), so English reads exactly as before. |
| API errors | Shown by code in the reader's language (`apps/web/src/i18n/errors.ts`), the server's message as the fallback for codes without words. English keeps the server's words (the back office reads them; often more precise), except "not found" and "no access", as before. |
| The back office | Stays literal English: `OpsApp` wraps itself in an English provider, components outside any provider read English, and the shared helpers (`runStatusLabel`, `shortTime`, `outcomeText`, …) take the reader's `I18n` as an optional last argument that defaults to English, so back-office callers are unchanged. No language picker there. `<html lang>` follows the language. |
| Picker | In the portal's user menu: "Language" (the team's or browser's language, or one of the six by its own name), and for the client admin the team's default. Each choice saves and reloads the session, so the whole portal changes at once. |
| Summaries | Skill and tool `summary` is a `LocalizedTemplate`: a string (English, as every stored version has) or `{ en, es?, fr?, "pt-BR"?, it?, de? }` with English required. `renderSummary(template, output, locale)` picks the language, else English. The operator skill editor edits English and keeps the other versions as they were. |
| Runs | A run reads the client's language once, inside its durable `run:load` step (a replay writes in the same language), into `RunInfo.language`. The executor renders summaries in it and writes its own timeline words (`done`, `waiting for approval`, …) from `TIMELINE_WORDS` in core; step labels stay as the operator wrote them. `runAgent` takes `language`: summaries render in it (a tool-mode skill's handler summary too), and for any language but English the system prompt asks for client-facing text in it, by the language's English name from the client's settings, never in the task input or a tool result, never from document text (invariant 9). English prompts are unchanged, so recorded fixtures still match. |
| Translations | Written with the code in the brand voice: Spanish with "usted", French with "vous", Portuguese with "você", Italian with "tu", German with "Sie". **They need a fluent speaker's review before a client sees them** (the five message files, `TIMELINE_WORDS` and the language instruction in `runAgent`). |
| Checked | The portal in Chromium in de, fr, pt-BR, es and it, light and dark, at 1440 and 1024 px, against the built app with a mocked API: no text overflowing its box, no truncated select. |
| Translation pass (2026-11-04) | Done for the screens M5, M11, Track B's open items and approver rules added: the portal's Settings (people, invites, emails, approvals), Reports and the activity report, the Service page, record pages and their Receipts, the shared document page, and "Who approves" (the portal's section and the client-admin and viewer side of the shared editor), with the shared people list and invite form. Report periods and counts go through `Intl` (`usage/format.ts` takes the reader's locale; the back office keeps en-US). Back-office-only text in shared components (the operator's rule editor and Try it, the ops document failure line, "already in this client") stays English. Core's tightening problems show by code. **The new strings need the fluent speaker's review too.** |
| Translation pass, texts to reach out (2026-11-04) | Also done for the screens texts and WhatsApp, the knowledge base, calls, Stripe billing and reach out added: the portal's sidebar links (Knowledge, Contacts, Billing, People), `/contacts` and the shared contacts panel (consent, opt-outs, details, preferred channels, quiet hours, the message and call list with its delivery and call statuses), `/knowledge` and the shared entry list and form, `/billing` with the shared credit meter and invoice table, and `/people` with the shared role editor. What contacts wrote, call transcripts, email text, role names and descriptions, entry titles and bodies stay as written, never translated. Billing prices go through `Intl` from their decimal strings (`fmt.unitPrice`, up to 4 places), counts and periods through the reader's locale; the back office's `price()` and `credits()` keep their English words. A contact's language shows by its name in the reader's language (`Intl.DisplayNames`). "Next channel after" is a number and a unit (minutes, hours, days) stored as the platform's duration, so no one types English units. Back-office-only screens (messaging and calls setup, email domain, the agent's channels, the ops billing pages and the knowledge tab's history) stay English. **The new strings need the fluent speaker's review too.** |
| New client's language | The new-client screen asks for the client's language (a select of the six, English by default) and sends it in the create body (`language`), stored as `tenant.settings.language` in the same insert. |

Open after stream I:
- A fluent speaker's review of the five translations (above), including the strings the translation pass added.
- Notifications and emails (M11) should use the client's language when they are sent.
- Text the server or a connector writes still reads in English in the portal: tool and connector descriptions (the inbox's change line, a run's "changes to your tools", connection cards, "Who approves"), a raw record's entity type, inbox titles the runtime writes for external actions and reach-out approvals ("Reach {name}: …"), the reach-out lines the server writes into a contact's message list ("Reached out by text …", "No reply in time …", "Not sent: …"), document validation `problems`, upload refusal reasons (other languages show a generic word), and field labels derived from record keys. Record values, document text, names and operator-written labels are data and stay as written.
- No connector or platform tool carries a summary per language yet; the skill editor edits only English.
- M5 has no route that removes a member yet. When one lands, it should also drop their `user_languages` entry.

### 1.19 Texts and calls: Twilio, WhatsApp, ElevenLabs (owner, 2026-11-04; planned)

Agents send and receive text messages and make and take phone calls, as tools they are given, drawing on what they know about the client. Planned here; not built yet. The defaults below stand unless the owner changes them (§7).

| Topic | Decision (default) |
|---|---|
| Connectors | `twilio`: SMS, voice, **phone numbers** (bought and released through Twilio's API in the client's subaccount, by the operator from the back office; never an agent tool, since a number costs money and agents can't change their own setup) and **WhatsApp first through Twilio's WhatsApp senders** on that number (owner, 2026-11-04): one message format, one webhook check and one log for SMS, WhatsApp and voice, with SMS as a fallback. `whatsapp_meta` comes after: Meta's WhatsApp Cloud API directly, so a client can use **its own number and its own WhatsApp Business Account**, with no per-message markup and Meta's newer features. Both give agents the same `whatsapp.*` tools, so a workflow doesn't change when a client moves from one to the other. `elevenlabs`: voice agents for live calls (speech in and out, low latency), on the client's Twilio number. |
| WhatsApp onboarding | **Through Twilio (first):** the operator registers the client's Twilio number as a WhatsApp sender under the client's business name (Twilio's self sign-up, with the client's Meta business verification). **Through Meta (after):** the client connects its own WhatsApp Business Account from the portal through Meta's Embedded Signup, like signing in to its other tools (the operator never holds its credentials); that needs Mezonas registered as a Meta Tech Provider (business verification and app review: start early, like Google's). Its number can be its own or the client's Twilio number (the platform asks Meta for the registration code and reads it from that number's SMS or call webhook). |
| Whose accounts | Mezonas' own Twilio account with a **subaccount and number per client**, provisioned by the operator; Mezonas' own ElevenLabs account. These are Mezonas' infrastructure (like email sending), not the client's tools; a client that already has Twilio can connect its own instead. WhatsApp is on the client's Twilio number first, and the client's own account once Meta is added (above). Usage (messages, minutes) is metered into `usage_daily` and counts against credits (§1.17). |
| Tools | Reads: `messages.search`, `message.get`, `call.get` (transcript, summary, recording link). Writes, each an external action behind approval: `sms.send`, `whatsapp.send` (approved templates outside WhatsApp's 24-hour window), `call.place` (a call with a stated purpose, recipient and the agent to speak). Events: `message.received`, `call.completed`, connector event types as `webhook.received` is; Twilio webhooks verified by their signature, and Meta's (later) by `X-Hub-Signature-256`. |
| Live calls | A call can't be approved sentence by sentence, so the approval is for **placing the call**: who, why, which agent and what it may do. During the call the voice agent may only use read tools and what the client knows; anything that would change a client's tool is proposed during the call and goes to the inbox after it. Inbound calls and replies to texts follow the same rule as any write: off unless the operator sets the step to run without approval, the matrix allows `auto` and the agent is in `act` mode (invariant 8). |
| What agents know | A **per-client knowledge base** the agent draws on: the client's documents, FAQs and policies, plus its memory notes, never another client's (invariant 1). Reached through tools (`knowledge.search`), so it is evidence with sources, not instructions. ElevenLabs gets the call's knowledge from our tools at call time, not a copy kept in its account. This is new work: the platform has memory notes and documents, not a knowledge base yet. |
| Untrusted text | Incoming texts, WhatsApp messages and call transcripts are untrusted data, like email (invariants 9 and 12): tool results only, stored per contact thread, the original kept. |
| Contacts and consent | A contact record per client (phone, WhatsApp, language, consent and its source, opt-out). Every send checks consent and quiet hours first; STOP and opt-out words are honoured at once; calls say they are an automated assistant and ask before recording. US texting needs A2P 10DLC registration per client number. These rules are checked in code, not left to the model. Needs a migration. |
| Languages | Texts and calls in the contact's language; translation of incoming messages comes with §1.18's next step. |
| Order | After the MVP connectors (QuickBooks, Gmail), approver rules and Stripe: contacts and consent, the knowledge base, then Twilio numbers, SMS and WhatsApp, then calls, then WhatsApp through Meta. |

### 1.20 Approver rules (built, 2026-11-04)

§1.17's approver rules: who approves a change to a client's tool. They decide **who**, never
**whether**: approval-on defaults, the connection matrix and act mode are untouched (invariant 8).

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Storage | `tenant.settings.approver_rules = {revision, rules}`; every save bumps the revision and writes a `tenant_change` row (`setting: approver_rules`, `side`, the rules `from` and `to`, the reason, the actor). No migration: 0012 stays unused. |
| A rule | `{id, match, approver, setBy, note?}` in `packages/core/src/approvals/rules.ts` (Zod, pure, shared by API, runtime and both editors), at most 50 per client. `match` has any of `connector`, `tool`, `verb` (create, update, delete), `amountOver {value, currency}` and `agentId`; every field given must hold, and a rule with none matches every change. `approver` is the client admin, a role (`client_user`: anyone in the client), one named client user, or the operator on the client's behalf. First match wins. |
| Amounts | A tool that carries money declares where in its input (`ToolSpec.amount {path, currencyPath?, currency?}`, dotted paths; `defineWriteTool({amount})`). The testkit's update declares `amount` in USD; no other connector does yet (QuickBooks' tools will). Amounts are compared with decimal.js, strictly over the threshold, and only in the same currency; an amount without a readable currency, or a tool without `amount`, never matches an amount rule. A JSON number in the input is read through its decimal text. |
| Precedence | The client's rules first, then the step's approver, then the agent's (its definition, then the client agent's override), then the client's default (`approvals.external_actions`), then the client admin. Rules come before every approver an agent names, so a client admin's tightening wins over an operator-set step or agent approver. |
| When it applies | The gate (`packages/agents` `callTool`) reads the rules fresh for every proposal (`RuntimeStore.approvalRouting`, which replaces `clientApprover`), so a tightening applies to the next approval at once, mid-run too. Approvals already waiting keep their assignee. A rule naming a person who has left the client routes to the client admin. |
| Snapshots | A run's `config` keeps the rules it started under (`approverRules`), beside the agent's bindings and approver. Each approval's inbox payload records what routed it: `approverRoute {source: rule, step, agent, client or default, ruleId, revision}`. Who approved is recorded as before (`external_action.approved_by`, `inbox_item.resolved_by`). `mayResolve` is unchanged: an item assigned to a person is theirs alone, not the admin's or the operator's. |
| Person steps | Rules match changes to a client's tool (connector, tool, verb, amount); person steps have none of these and keep their own assignee. |
| Who sets them | The operator, for the clients they serve: `GET/PUT /api/ops/clients/:id/approver-rules` (others 404), any change, with a reason. The client admin: `PUT /api/approver-rules`, stricter only, checked on the server with core's `clientTighteningProblems` (403 `loosening` with the problems). Client users read them (`GET /api/approver-rules`). A save over a newer revision is 409 `stale`; saving nothing new is 409 `no_op`; a rule naming someone who isn't the client's, another client's agent, an unknown connector or write tool, a tool and connector or verb that disagree, or an amount on a tool without one is 422 `invalid_rule`. |
| Stricter only | Strictness: the operator 0, a role (anyone in the client) 1, the client admin or one named person 2. The client admin may raise a rule's approver to the same or a higher level (taking an approval back from the operator, narrowing to the admin or a person, moving between people) and add new rules, anywhere, naming the admin or one person. Never: removing, reordering or re-matching a rule (anyone's), or naming the operator. Because rules come first, a new level-2 rule can only make any change's approver stricter. The server keeps `setBy` and makes ids for new rules. |
| Screens | One editor (`apps/web/src/approvals/ApproverRules.tsx`) for both surfaces, like the matrix. Back office: the client page's "Who approves" section (ordered list, add, edit, up and down, remove, reason, change log, and "Try it with a sample change", which runs core's `routeApproval` over the unsaved rules, the agent's approver and the client's default). Portal: a "Who approves" section on Connections, under the matrix: the client admin picks only the same or a stricter approver per rule and adds rules naming the admin or one person; client users read them. The portal calls the operator's side "we" and never names the operator's people. |


### 1.21 Decisions made in the knowledge base stream (2026-11-04)

§1.19's "What agents know": a per-client knowledge base that agents (voice agents later) reach with `knowledge.search`. Migration 0007.

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| What it holds | The client's FAQs and policies (plain text with a title, written by the operator or the client admin), its documents (name, email subject and sender, and the latest extraction's header and lines), and the asking agent's memory notes. Per client only (forced RLS on every table, queries filter by `tenant_id`). |
| Entries and history | `knowledge_entry` is an entry's current state (kind, active or archived, latest version number); `knowledge_entry_version` keeps every saved title and text with its author and role, immutable (trigger). An edit, an archive, and a restore (editing an archived entry) each insert the next version. An edit names its `baseVersion`; a newer one on the server refuses it (409 `stale`). An unchanged edit saves nothing. |
| Index | `knowledge_chunk`: passages (paragraphs packed to about 1,200 characters, long ones split at sentence ends) with a `tsvector` (title weighted above text) and a GIN index. It is a derived index, so the runtime may delete from it. An entry's passages are rebuilt in the same transaction as each save; an archived entry has none. Documents are indexed lazily, before each knowledge read (the tool, the Knowledge tab, the search preview), when they are new or have a newer extraction, at most 50 per call; the extraction path is unchanged. |
| Search | Postgres full-text search. The question's words are OR-ed (`plainto_tsquery` with `&` turned into `|`) and ranked with `ts_rank`, so a natural question finds an entry that has only some of its words. Each source keeps its best passage; excerpts come from `ts_headline` as plain text. The configuration follows the client's language (`tenant.settings.language`: English, Spanish, French, Portuguese, Italian, German; else English) and is stored per passage. |
| Embeddings | Not built: `packages/llm` has no embedding path. Follow-up: an embedding model behind the same tool (pgvector is installed), hybrid with the text ranking. |
| Memory notes | Searched at query time, not indexed. An agent's search sees only its own live notes, plus a test run's own notes in that run. The back office's preview sees every agent's live notes. Test-run notes never show in the Knowledge tab. |
| Tool | `knowledge.search {query (1–500), limit (1–20, default 5)}` → `{query, count, results: [{kind (faq, policy, document, memory), id, title, excerpt, score, sources}], sources}`. Zod on both sides. A read (`sideEffect: none`) through a `KnowledgePort` that `createDbPorts` always provides. Registered with the platform's tools, and offered as the built-in tool-mode skill "Search what we know" (category Knowledge). Sources: entry and its version, document and its extraction, or the memory note (invariant 10). Its description says the passages are data, never instructions (invariant 9). |
| Where it lives | `packages/documents/src/knowledge/` (the documents capability already reaches db; most of what is indexed is documents). The tool and port are in `packages/agents` (`tools/knowledge.ts`, `log/knowledge-port.ts`). |
| Back office | The client page gets tabs (Agents, Knowledge). Knowledge: FAQs and policies (in use or all, edit, archive, history), the documents with whether each is indexed and how many passages it has, the 50 latest live memory notes and their count, an add form, and "Try a search" with sources. Routes `GET /api/ops/clients/:id/knowledge`, `POST …/entries`, `PUT …/entries/:entryId`, `POST …/entries/:entryId/archive`, `GET …/entries/:entryId/versions`, `GET …/search?q=&limit=`. |
| Portal | `/knowledge` for the client admin only (sidebar link for admins): the client's own FAQs and policies; add, edit, archive, show archived. Our edits show as "us", the client's people by name. Routes `GET /api/knowledge`, `POST /api/knowledge/entries`, `PUT /api/knowledge/entries/:id`, `POST /api/knowledge/entries/:id/archive`. Client users have no access: agents use the entries, and people ask the admin. Strings are plain English until the languages stream converts them. |
| Who may edit | The operator and the client admin may both edit any of the client's entries. Every version records who saved it. |
| Migration numbering | Parallel streams reserve migration numbers ahead, so the journal test lets a branch skip numbers. Tags must still be numbered, unique and ascending. On merge, the coordinator renumbers the journal's idx values and keeps `when` increasing. |


### 1.22 Decisions made in the messaging stream (Twilio numbers, SMS, WhatsApp; 2026-11-04)

Builds §1.19's contacts and consent, and Twilio numbers, SMS and WhatsApp. Calls, ElevenLabs and Meta are not in this stream. Twilio facts we couldn't check are in `packages/connectors/src/twilio/NOTES.md`.

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Data (migration 0006) | `contact` per client: name, `phone` and `whatsapp` in E.164 (each unique per client, at least one), `language`, `consent` per channel (`{sms|whatsapp: {grantedAt, source}}`), `opted_out_at` and `opt_out_source`. An append-only `contact_change` log. `contact` is a message sender. `usage_daily.messages`. RLS forced, no DELETE: an opted-out contact stays, so the opt-out keeps holding. |
| Threads | A contact's messages are a `thread` with subject `contact`. Inbound messages are sender `contact`; ours are `agent` with the sending version's `agent_version_id`. The thread is for people to read: no model reads it (invariants 9 and 12). |
| Numbers | E.164 only. Typed spaces, brackets and `00` are cleaned up, but a number without its country code is refused rather than guessed. |
| Consent | Per channel, with how it was given (free text, ≤ 200 characters). Changing a channel's number withdraws that channel's consent. An opt-out stops every channel. Granting consent while opted out is refused. |
| STOP and START | Matched on the whole message (any case, trailing punctuation ignored), or taken from Twilio's `OptOutType`. STOP, STOPALL, UNSUBSCRIBE, CANCEL, END, QUIT, REVOKE and OPTOUT opt out at once, in the webhook's transaction. START, UNSTOP and YES lift the opt-out and record consent for that channel ("texted START"). A STOP opt-out is lifted only by the contact's START. An opt-out a person recorded can be lifted by a person. |
| Unknown senders | Someone who isn't a contact becomes one (named by their WhatsApp profile name or number) with no consent, so their thread and any STOP are kept. |
| Quiet hours | Always on, per client, in `tenant.timezone`. The default is 21:00–08:00, and the operator can move it (`tenant.settings.messaging.quietHours`). The approval card is drawn at any hour. The send itself is refused in quiet hours: the action fails with "send it again after that". There is no queue. |
| Checks in code | `sms.send` and `whatsapp.send` ask `ctx.services.messaging` (apps/api, over the contacts, one withTenant per call) before Twilio is called: the number must be a contact of this client, with consent for the channel, not opted out, and outside quiet hours. A sender number must exist. US texts need A2P approved. The same checks run when the approval is proposed (quiet hours aside), so a refused send never reaches the inbox. Without the services nothing is sent. |
| Connector services | `ConnectionContext.services` (new, optional) carries platform services into connector calls; `RuntimeDeps.services` provides them. Connectors still never read the database themselves. |
| Shared namespaces | `ConnectorManifest.sharedNamespaces` lets a connector's tools and events use `sms.`, `whatsapp.`, `messages.` and `message.` besides `twilio.`. So the ids `sms.send`, `whatsapp.send`, `messages.search`, `message.get`, `message.received` and `message.status` are provider-neutral (`connectors/src/messaging/spec.ts`), and Meta's connector will supply the same ids. The tool registry still refuses an id registered twice. When Meta arrives, the registry resolves a shared id by the agent's binding. |
| Whose Twilio | Mezonas' main account (`TWILIO_ACCOUNT_SID`, `TWILIO_AUTH_TOKEN`, deployment secrets) only makes subaccounts. Each client's subaccount SID and auth token are the encrypted credentials of its `twilio` connection (auth `api_key`, made by the operator, `connected_by` the operator). Config holds the subaccount SID, numbers, A2P and the webhook URLs. A disconnected client keeps its subaccount: reconnecting reads the token back with the main account. |
| Numbers (operator only) | `/api/ops/clients/:id/messaging/*`: search US local numbers that take texts, buy one in the client's subaccount with its `SmsUrl` set to our webhook, release it, and mark it as the WhatsApp sender once registered in Twilio's console. Never an agent tool. Every change goes to `connection_change`. |
| A2P 10DLC | Shown and tracked, not automated. The operator records the brand (BN…) and Messaging Service (MG…); "Check status" reads both. It is approved when the brand is `APPROVED` and the campaign `VERIFIED`. **P-8:** registered by Twilio's API from the client's details, with the fees passed on (§1.70); recording SIDs by hand stays for a client registered in Twilio's console. |
| WhatsApp | Free text inside 24 hours of the contact's last message (from our inbound records). Outside that only a template (`template.id` = Twilio Content SID, with variables) may go out; Twilio enforces that the template is approved. |
| Once per key | A send is an external action (approval by default, invariant 8). A re-run after a crash (the action left `executing`) first looks for a message already recorded under the action's key and returns it, in `before` and in the send, **before** the consent, opt-out and quiet-hours checks: an opt-out since then can't record a message Twilio sent as failed. The checks still stop every message not yet sent, and every new send. |
| At most once | Twilio's message create takes no idempotency key (NOTES.md). So the send records its attempt (raw record `MessageAttempt`, by the action's key: who, from which number, what, when) before calling Twilio, and a re-run that finds an attempt but no message never sends again. It lists Twilio's messages from our number to the contact (`GET Messages.json?To=&From=`) and keeps the outbound ones created since the attempt (two minutes' leeway for clocks) that no other action recorded. Exactly one: adopted as this send (recorded, threaded and counted once). None or several: the action fails with "… may have been sent; check before trying again" and the operator gets a `review` inbox item with the candidates. The connector throws `UnconfirmedWriteError`; `executeAction` fails the action and opens the item in one transaction, so a retry neither sends nor opens a second item. |
| Retried automatic steps | The gate (`callTool`) looks up the action by the step's next idempotency key before any proposal check (the matrix, the before state and the connector's checks) and resumes or returns it; the checks apply only to a new action. A step that ran without approval and was retried after a crash used to fail on an opt-out and leave its action `executing`. The approval path, which executes the action directly, is unchanged. |
| Webhooks | `POST /api/twilio/:tenantId/messages` and `/status`, public. The path's client scopes the lookup. The `AccountSid` must be the client's subaccount and `To` one of its numbers. `X-Twilio-Signature` is checked under the subaccount's token over the URL we gave Twilio (stored), not the request URL. Failures are 403 with nothing to probe. Messages are stored once as raw records (`Message`, by SID; `MessageStatus`, by SID and status), so retries and replays do nothing. The answer is an empty TwiML `<Response>`. |
| Events | `message.received` (contact, channel, SID, raw record, keyword; **no text**: a step reads it with `message.get`, so it reaches a model only as a tool result) and `message.status`. Both can trigger workflows. |
| Usage | `usage_daily.messages`: +1 per message Twilio accepted, once per SID, for the client agent whose run sent it (UTC day). The rollup leaves the column alone. Inbound messages aren't counted. |
| Screens | Back office: `/ops/clients/:id/messaging` (subaccount, numbers, WhatsApp sender, A2P, quiet hours, contacts), linked from the client page. Portal: `/contacts` for the client admin (add, record or withdraw consent, opt out, see the messages). |
| Not built | Voice and ElevenLabs (calls stream), Meta's WhatsApp, MMS media download, numbers outside the US, toll-free verification, Messaging Services by API, translation of incoming messages, counting inbound messages. |

### 1.23 Decisions made in the calls stream (Twilio voice, ElevenLabs voice agents; 2026-11-04)

Builds §1.19's "Live calls": agents place and take phone calls on the client's Twilio number (§1.22's subaccount and numbers), with an ElevenLabs voice agent speaking. Facts we couldn't check (both APIs, signatures, limits, prices) are in `packages/connectors/src/elevenlabs/NOTES.md`.

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Data (migration 0008, own branch and PR #32) | Only `usage_daily.call_seconds`. Calls are raw records on the client's Twilio connection, each stored once by its id: `CallRequest` (our call id), `Call` (CA…, with who answered and why), `CallSession`, `CallStatus` (CA…:status), `CallRecording` (voicemail), `CallFollowUp`, `CallToolCall`, `CallTranscript` (by conversation id) and `VoiceAgent` (by settings hash). People read calls in the contact's thread (channel `voice`); no model reads it. |
| Tools and events | Shared namespace `call.` on the `twilio` connector: `call.place` (external, create, behind approval), `call.answer` (external, create; records an answer that happened when the call rang) and `call.get` (read; becomes a read skill as connector reads do). Events: `call.received` (with the decision) and `call.completed` (outcome, length, follow-up count; no words). |
| ElevenLabs | Mezonas' own account (`ELEVENLABS_API_KEY`, `ELEVENLABS_WEBHOOK_SECRET`). It is a module of `@countersign/connectors`, not a registered connector: clients never connect it and agents never call it. We don't import numbers into ElevenLabs. Twilio keeps asking our webhooks, and we hand each call over with register-call, so the approval and the inbound decision stay ours. |
| Voice agent settings | From the step in the agent's version (invariant 4): `purpose`, `firstMessage`, `voiceId`, `tools` (read tools) and `maxMinutes` (1–30, default 10) on `call.place` / `call.answer`. The prompt is the platform's voice rules, then the version's identity and instructions, then the purpose. The language is the contact's, else the client's (§1.18 languages; English otherwise). There is one ElevenLabs agent per distinct config, made on first use and kept by its hash (`VoiceAgent`). |
| Opening | Fixed in code, in each portal language (marked for review): "this is an automated assistant calling for {client}", then the version's own line, then "May I keep a written record of this call?". |
| Purpose and opening are fixed text (invariant 9) | `purpose` and `firstMessage` become the voice agent's instructions and opening line, so they come only from text written in the step in the agent's version. A tool spec lists such fields in `fixedInputs` (`call.place` and `call.answer` list both). The gate (`callTool`) compares them with the action step's input as written, before templates (`StepContext.literalInput`, set by the executor's action port). It refuses the call before any approval card or write when a field was filled from a template (`{{trigger.*}}`, `{{steps.*.output.*}}`), changed after the step, or written by a model (a skill or agent mode has no step input). This way, what a contact texted or said (read with `message.get` or `call.get`) can never steer a call, including a step set to `not_required`. Workflow validation flags the same thing (`input_not_fixed`: a templated field on an action step, or a skill whose tools include such a tool), so publishing is refused too. Inbound answering reads the step's raw input and forwards or sends to voicemail if a field is templated. Other fields (`to`, `tools`) may still be templates. We chose refusal over quoting the purpose into the prompt as delimited data: refusal holds whichever path filled the field. |
| Re-run of a placed call | When an action left `executing` runs again after a crash, `call.place` returns the call already placed under its key before any consent, opt-out or quiet-hours check, in both `before` and `run`. This means an opt-out since then can't record a call Twilio placed as failed. Every call not yet placed is still checked. |
| At most one call | Twilio's call create takes no idempotency key (NOTES.md). The call's request (`CallRequest`, with the action's key and `requestedAt`) is stored by `prepareOutbound` before Twilio is called, and is the attempt. A re-run that finds a request but no placed call never places again, and skips the checks as above: it lists Twilio's calls from our number to the contact (`GET Calls.json?To=&From=`) and keeps the outbound ones created since the request (two minutes' leeway for clocks) that no other call record has. Exactly one: adopted, recorded under the request's call id so Twilio's webhooks for it find it. None or several: the action fails with "The call to … may have been placed; check before trying again" and the operator gets a `review` inbox item with the candidates (`UnconfirmedWriteError`, as for messages in §1.22). |
| Approval | `call.place` is approved as a whole: the card shows who, why (purpose), from which number, the agent (the item's agent), the tools it may use and the time limit. Consent and opt-out are checked when the card is drawn, and again with quiet hours when the call is placed. A refused call never reaches the inbox. During quiet hours the action fails ("place it again after that"); nothing is queued. |
| Consent | Calls need their own consent channel, `voice` (to the contact's phone). Agreeing to texts isn't agreeing to calls. Changing the phone withdraws it, an opt-out (including STOP) stops calls too, and calling in isn't consent. |
| During the call | The voice agent's server tools are the step's tools that read, and that are also the agent's own tools (extraTools or skills), plus `propose_follow_up`. The tool webhook `POST /api/voice/:tenantId/tools/:tool` needs the call's id and token (HMAC of client and call under a key derived from the webhook secret: no storage), stops working once the transcript has arrived or the time limit plus 5 minutes has passed, and refuses any write whatever the step lists. Then the tool runs through `callTool` (the gate) as the call's client agent version, with its bindings and the connection's matrix. Tool use is kept as `CallToolCall` raw records (a call has no step run of its own). |
| Changes asked for on a call | Never made during the call. `propose_follow_up` notes what was asked. After the call, each note becomes a `review` inbox item for the approver (step, agent, client setting, else the client admin), with the follow-up and the transcript record as sources, and no run (so nothing resumes or re-sends). A person makes the change. |
| Inbound calls | `POST /api/twilio/:tenantId/voice`. The client agent's answering step is the first active client agent bound to this Twilio connection with a live workflow on `call.received` whose steps include `call.answer` (oldest agent first). It answers live only when `decideToolCall` says the step runs without approval: the step is `not_required`, the matrix allows `auto` for create, and the agent is in `act` mode (invariant 8). ElevenLabs must also be set up. Otherwise the call is forwarded to the operator's forward number, or goes to voicemail. The decision and its reason are stored with the call and written in the contact's thread, then `call.received` carries them. In the run that starts, `call.answer` records the answer once, automatically; if the agent didn't answer, its before-state check fails with the reason, so no approval card is drawn after the fact. A retried ring answers the same way without deciding again. If ElevenLabs fails while the call rings, the caller still reaches voicemail. |
| Voicemail and forward | Voicemail is a polite greeting in the client's language, a tone, up to 2 minutes (`<Record>`), stored once as `CallRecording`. Forward is `<Dial>` to the operator-set number for 20 seconds. Neither is an agent's usage. Playing voicemails in the portal isn't built. |
| After the call | ElevenLabs' post-call webhook (`/api/elevenlabs/post-call`) is checked by its signature with a 30-minute window. It is matched to our call by `call_id`/`tenant_id` dynamic variables and the voice agent's id, and stored once per conversation. The transcript and summary are untrusted data (tool results only, through `call.get`), kept only if the person said yes to a record (`record_ok` data collection). Otherwise nothing of what was said is kept. No audio is kept (`record_voice: false`). A line goes in the contact's thread, and `call.completed` is emitted. Calls the voice agent never spoke on (voicemail, forward, no answer, busy, failed) are completed from Twilio's final status. |
| Usage | `usage_daily.call_seconds` += Twilio's `CallDuration` of each finished call an agent placed or answered, once (the final status is stored once first), for that client agent, by UTC day. The rollup leaves it alone. |
| Setup (operator only) | `/api/ops/clients/:id/calls`. Turn calls on (our voice webhook URLs stored in `connection.config.voice`), take a number's calls (sets its `VoiceUrl` and `StatusCallback` at Twilio), set the forward number, see who answers now (the same check) and the recent calls. Every change goes to `connection_change`. Turning calls off clears the numbers first. The Calls panel is on the client's Texts and WhatsApp page. |
| Isolation | Every webhook's path names the client and scopes the lookup (`withTenant`). Twilio's signature uses that client's subaccount token, and the tool token is bound to the client and the call. Another client's path gets 403 or 401. |
| Network inside a transaction | The voice webhooks call ElevenLabs (make the agent, hand the call over) inside the request's one `withTenant` transaction, within Twilio's ~15-second answer window. This is accepted to keep one connection per request; it is a follow-up if it holds connections too long. |
| Not built | Recording audio, playing voicemails, answering-machine detection, transfer to a person mid-call, deleting unused ElevenLabs agents, choosing the voice agent's LLM, SHAKEN/STIR and CNAM registration (Twilio console), numbers outside the US, translation of transcripts (§1.18 next step). |

### 1.24 Decisions made in stream J (Stripe billing)

> **Superseded in part by §1.32 (2026-11-05):** Mezonas now works out each bill and Stripe only
> bills it. The subscription, base quantity, meter, usage report, price-id and subscription-webhook
> rows below no longer apply; credits, unit costs, rounding, periods, collection and secrets still do.

Owner, 2026-11-04 (§1.17): each client is a Stripe customer; a monthly base fee per active client agent includes an allowance of credits; usage beyond it is billed per credit; one credit is one cent of what the work cost us (model cost plus provider costs), converted with unit costs kept as operator settings; the markup is in the overage price. migration 0009 (its own PR, #34).

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Where the records live | A client's Stripe ids (customer, subscription, the base and overage items), the base quantity Stripe has, the subscription status and the last invoice's outcome are in `tenant.settings.billing` (identifiers, no money). The tenant table is global, so the webhook finds a client by customer id without a client scope; updates merge into the key in one statement (`jsonb_set`). What can't go there is in migration 0009. Per client, with forced RLS: `billing_usage_report` holds one row per client, period and day, with money as `numeric(14,4)` plus currency; only status, error and sent_at change, and rows are never deleted. Also per client: `billing_event`, which stores each Stripe event once. Global, read by every session and written only when `app.operator` is on: `billing_setting` (one row) and `billing_unit_cost`. |
| Settings | All placeholders until the owner sets prices. Allowance: 2,000 credits per active agent ($20 of our cost). Unit costs: 1.0000 per USD of `cost_usd`, 0.0083 per message, 0.1000 per 60 call seconds. Meter event name: `mezonas_credits`. Invoices due in 30 days. No Stripe price ids yet. The operator edits them in `/ops/billing`. When the price ids are saved they are checked against Stripe: the base price must be licensed and monthly, the overage price metered and monthly, both in the same currency. Their amounts are kept as snapshots for the screens. Without a Stripe key, the ids are saved unchecked and no amounts are shown. |
| Credit conversion | Generic over measures: a measure is a `usage_daily` column (by its SQL name), and the unit cost setting for that name prices it (`unit_cost` per `per_units`). Every column except the key is a measure. A column a later migration adds (`messages`, `call_seconds` from the texts and calls branches) counts as soon as the Drizzle schema has it and a unit cost exists. No code names a measure. Tested with a `widgets` column added to the test database. |
| Rounding | Credits are integers. A client's day is priced across all its agents and measures, then rounded up once to whole cents (any work at all is at least one credit). The period's credits are the sum of its days. Everything is decimal.js; nothing passes through a float. |
| Allowance | Pooled across a client's agents: per-agent allowance × active client agents when the day is reported. Overage is the credits beyond it. Each report sends only the increment over what was reported earlier in the period, never a negative one: an allowance that grows mid-period (a new agent) takes nothing back. |
| Active agents | Client agents with run status `active` (not paused, retired, drafts, discarded or test copies). A paused agent is not billed until it is activated again (open for the owner). |
| Periods | Calendar months in UTC, like `usage_daily`'s days. Subscriptions are anchored to midnight UTC on the 1st of next month (`billing_cycle_anchor`, prorated first invoice), so Stripe's periods match ours. |
| Collection | `collection_method=send_invoice` with `days_until_due` from the settings. Stripe emails the invoice with its hosted page. v1 has no card-on-file flow. |
| Quantity | The base item's quantity is kept equal to the client's active agents. After activate, pause and retire (deferred until after the response), and on the hourly pass as a safety net. It is set as an absolute value, so a repeat or a race lands on the same number. Changes prorate. |
| Usage reporting | On the cron's hourly pass at the usage sweep's minute (:07 UTC, after the rollup). Per client set up for billing: sync the quantity, report yesterday (UTC), then retry unsent reports from the last 30 days. Steps: (1) insert the day's row (`pending`, or `none` with nothing to send) with identifier `mezonas:<tenant>:<period>:<day>`; (2) send a Stripe billing meter event under that identifier, which is also the Idempotency-Key, timestamped at the day's last second; (3) mark the row sent or failed. A day already reported sends nothing new. A pending or failed row is resent under the same identifier. Usage that changes after its day is reported is caught by the next day's increment. The day after a month ends reports that month's last day at 00:07 UTC (Stripe's grace period is to check, NOTES.md). |
| Test runs | `usage_daily` includes test runs of a client's agents, so they are billed to that client. A template's Sandbox test copies are in the Sandbox client, which is not billed. Open for the owner. |
| Webhook | `POST /api/billing/stripe/webhook`, public. The `Stripe-Signature` header (HMAC-SHA256 over `t.payload`) is checked on the raw body with a 300-second tolerance in both directions, then the event is parsed with Zod. Handled: `invoice.paid`, `invoice.payment_failed`, `customer.subscription.updated`, `customer.subscription.deleted`. The client is found by customer id. Each event is stored once per event id (a repeat answers `duplicate`). A subscription event older than the last one applied changes nothing. Other events, and customers we don't bill, are acknowledged and dropped. Bodies over 256 KB are refused. |
| Stripe client | `packages/connectors/src/stripe/`: form-encoded requests, injected `FetchLike`, Zod on responses, `StripeError`. No connector manifest and no agent tools: billing is Mezonas' own infrastructure. `fakeStripe` is in `@countersign/connectors/testing`. What could not be checked against Stripe's docs (blocked here) is in its `NOTES.md`. No `Stripe-Version` is sent, so the account default applies (open). |
| Back office | `/ops/billing` (sidebar: Billing) shows each client's period so far (agents, base fee, credits used, allowance, extra, our cost, billing status) with a Set up button, and the settings form (allowance, days until due, price ids, meter event name, unit costs per measure; a priced measure the rollup doesn't have yet says so). The client page has a Billing section: set up with an optional billing email, the plan and our cost, the credit meter, Sync now, this period's daily reports and the invoices with links. Routes: `GET /api/ops/billing`, `PUT /api/ops/billing/settings`, `GET /api/ops/billing/clients/:id`, `POST …/setup`, `POST …/sync`, all operator-only (the contract test covers the 403s). |
| Portal | `/billing`, for the client admin only (`GET /api/billing`, `client_admin`), shows: the plan (services × base fee, the monthly total), credits included each month (shared by its services), extra credits and their price, this period's credits as a meter, a notice when the last payment failed, and Stripe's invoices with the hosted page ("View and pay" while open) and PDF. Never our cost, tokens or prompts. Credits are the billing unit, so the field names avoid the forbidden list (`creditsUsed`, `extraCredits`, `extraCreditPrice`). The contract test and a test that walks the response keys check this. Strings are plain English for stream I to convert. |
| Secrets | `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY` and `STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET` are Worker secrets (`docs/operations.md`), empty in `.env.example`. With no key, billing is off: the cron skips it, set-up answers 409, and the screens say Stripe isn't connected. |


### 1.25 Reach out: agents contacting people (owner, 2026-11-04; design)

An agent knows the people it works with and reaches out to them by email, text, WhatsApp or call, in their language, under conditions the operator sets. Builds on contacts (§1.22), calls (§1.23), approver rules (§1.20) and languages (§1.18).

| Topic | Design |
|---|---|
| Contacts | §1.22's contact gains: **email**, **preferred channels in order** (e.g. WhatsApp, then call), **language** (already there), **timezone** and quiet hours (default: the client's), **roles** (labels such as `purchaser`, `owner`, `vendor rep`), consent **per channel** (email added beside texts, WhatsApp and calls), and notes. Client data: per client only (invariant 1); the operator and the client admin edit them. |
| An agent's people | A template declares the **roles** it reaches (`people: [{role, description, required, channels}]` in the bundle, no contact data). A client agent **binds each role to one or more contacts**, like connection bindings: operational data on the client agent, never in a version, so forks and promotions never carry anyone's details (invariant 11). Activation refuses a required role with nobody bound. This is how "add people to an agent's memory" works: who they are, how to reach them and when. |
| Conditions | Per binding, checked in code before anything is sent: **urgency → channels** (default: urgent: call, then text; normal: the contact's preferred order; FYI: email only), **hours** (the contact's timezone and quiet hours; urgent may be allowed outside them only if the operator says so), **how often** (at most N per day and one open reach-out per contact and subject), **only if** (an optional rule over the step's facts, the same rules as forks, e.g. amount over $500), and **escalation** (no reply after a set time → the next channel; stop at the first reply, an opt-out or the last channel). |
| The skill | `comms.reach-out`, built in. Input: `to` (a role or a contact), `about` (the record it concerns, as sources), `purpose` (fixed text, §1.23's fixed-input rule), `message` (a fixed template per language, or "compose": the model writes it from the fixed purpose and the record's data, in the contact's language), `urgency`, `expectReply`, `timeout`. Output: `outcome` (`replied`, `no_reply`, `declined`, `opted_out`, `not_allowed`), the channel used, the reply as untrusted data, the contact, and sources. |
| Waiting for a reply | The step waits like a person step: the run waits for the reply event (`message.received`, `call.completed`, an email reply) or the timeout, using the same wait the inbox uses; escalation steps happen inside the wait. The workflow then forks on `outcome`. |
| Channel choice | The contact's preferred order, filtered by the binding's allowed channels, consent, what the client has connected and the hours; the first that passes is used; none passes → `not_allowed`, recorded with the reason, nothing sent. |
| Language | The contact's language, else the client's, else English. Fixed templates carry one text per language (as summaries do, §1.18); composed messages are written in that language. Replies in another language are translated when §1.18's message translation lands. |
| Approval | On by default (invariant 8). One approval covers **the reach-out plan**: who, why, which channels and how many attempts; each attempt inside the plan then runs. Approver rules (§1.20) route these approvals like any other change. A step runs without approval only under the usual three conditions. |
| Evidence and safety | Every attempt and reply goes in the contact's thread with the run, the agent version and the sources (invariant 10). Replies and transcripts are data, never instructions (invariant 9). Consent, opt-out, hours and caps are code, not model judgement. Test runs never send. |
| The agent's own channels (owner, 2026-11-04) | An agent is **given channels**, as it is given connections: each client agent binds an email channel, a texting number, a WhatsApp number and a calling number, each to a connection. Email can be **a mailbox of its own** (a Gmail account made for the agent, which the client or the operator signs in to), **Mezonas' sending service** (Resend, from an address on the client's verified domain, our domain until then), or **a person's Gmail** that the person signs in to and lets the agent send from. Texts and calls use the client's Twilio numbers (§1.22, §1.23). The skill sends only through the agent's bound channels; an unbound channel is `not_allowed`. |
| Email sending | Gmail gains send (`gmail.send`, Google's send scope, behind approval like every send; this adds to the Gmail app review) and still never syncs beyond the connection's query (invariant 12). A `resend` connector is Mezonas' infrastructure: a domain per client verified by DNS records shown in the back office. Replies come back to the client's forwarding address (§1.6) or the Gmail mailbox and are matched to the reach-out by thread and headers; reply text is untrusted data. |
| Who edits (owner, 2026-11-04) | **The operator and the client admin, fully**: both add and edit contacts, bind people to an agent's roles and set or loosen the conditions (hours, how often, urgency → channels, escalation, "only if"). Every change is logged with who (the tenant_change pattern). What stays the operator's alone: whether a step needs approval and the connection matrix (invariant 8), and binding channels to connections. Consent and opt-outs are never overridden by either. |

#### Decisions made while building reach out (stream K, 2026-11-04)

Built on `claude/reach-out` (based on `claude/voice-calls`). Facts about Resend we couldn't check are in `packages/connectors/src/resend/NOTES.md`.

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Approver rules (merge train, 2026-11-04) | A reach-out plan's approval goes through the approver rules (§1.20) like every other change: it is matched as a create through its first channel's send tool (e.g. `sms.send` on `twilio`) for this agent, with no amount; then the step's, the agent's and the client's default approver, then the client admin. |
| Data (migration 0010, own branch and PR #36) | `contact` gains `email` (lower case, unique per client), `preferred_channels`, `timezone` and `quiet_hours` (null means the client's), `roles` and `notes`. `contact_reachable_check` now also allows a contact with only an email. Email consent is a new key (`email`) in the existing `consent` jsonb. `agent` gains `people` (role → `{contactIds, conditions}`) and `channels` (channel → connection id), both operational columns like `bindings`. `reach_out` holds one row per run step: its plan, attempts, the reply (as untrusted data), outcome, due and next-attempt times, and `run_received_at`. RLS is forced, and there is no DELETE. A partial unique index allows one open reach-out per contact and subject. The journal's `when` leaves room for 0016. |
| Contacts | Changing an email withdraws email consent, as changing a number does for texts. Every edit logs `changed: {field: {before, after}}` in `contact_change` with who made it. Timezones are checked against the runtime's list. The operator and the client admin edit the same fields. Consent and opt-outs keep §1.22's rules: a binding never grants consent, and nothing overrides an opt-out. |
| People in the bundle | `people` is optional with no default, so older versions parse and hash unchanged (invariant 3). Validation refuses a duplicate role (`duplicate_role`). Diff and three-way merge treat each role as an entity (`person:<role>` in the list `bundle>people`), so roles added on both sides merge and one role changed two ways conflicts field by field. A bundle without `people` rebuilds without it. |
| Bindings | `setPeople` checks that every role is declared by the agent's live version (else its latest) and that every contact belongs to this client. `setChannels` checks that each connection is this client's and that its connector can carry the channel: `resend` carries email; `twilio` carries sms, whatsapp and voice. Gmail and Meta are listed as planned and refused with "isn't built yet". Both are logged in `agent_change` (`kind: people` with `by`, or `kind: channels`). People routes exist under `/api/ops/agents/:id/people` and in the portal at `/api/people`. Channels routes exist only under `/api/ops`, so a client admin gets 403. Activation refuses a required role with nobody bound. |
| The evaluator | `packages/core/src/reach/rules.ts` is pure. Checks run in order: opt-out (→ `opted_out`), the only-if rule (the fork rules, over `{trigger, input}`), one open reach-out per contact and subject, the daily cap (default 3, UTC day, not counting `not_allowed`), then urgency's channels filtered by the binding, the role's channels, the agent's bound channels (excluding a connection whose `create` is off), consent and address, and hours. The first channel that passes goes now. A later channel blocked only by quiet hours stays in the plan for escalation. Nothing passing → `not_allowed` with the reasons per channel. |
| Hours | Quiet hours apply to texts, WhatsApp and calls in the contact's timezone, and email goes at any hour. Urgent may go outside the contact's hours only when the binding allows it. The client's own quiet hours still hold at Twilio's sends (§1.22), so "urgent outside hours" can't send a text or call during the client's quiet hours. An attempt that falls in quiet hours is held until they end (`next_attempt_at`), not skipped. |
| Normal with no preferences | Least intrusive first: email, text, WhatsApp, call. |
| Subject | The `about` ids, sorted (`about:<ids>`), else the purpose. |
| Who is reached | A role's contacts are tried in the order they were bound, and the first whose plan passes is reached (one contact per reach-out). A contact named directly is reached under its role binding's conditions if it is bound to one of the agent's roles, else under the defaults. |
| The skill | `comms.reach-out` is built in, tool mode, with a handler (`comms.reach_out`) that refuses to run anywhere but a workflow step. apps/api's agent port routes the skill to the reach-out service by id. `SkillDefinition` gains `fixedInputs` and `reachesPeople` (built-ins only). `purpose` is a fixed input: validation flags a templated one (`input_not_fixed`), and the service refuses it at the step, before any plan, approval or send, as the gate does for `call.place`. `reachesPeople` gives the step an approval setting, although the skill lists no tools. Bindings to the matrix are checked per channel at plan time and at every attempt. |
| Approval | One inbox item (`kind: approval`) shows the plan: who, role, purpose, urgency, channels, attempts, escalation, language and the message. It is assigned like any write's approver: the step's, then the agent's, then the client agent's, then the client's setting. Approver rules (§1.17/§1.20) aren't built on this base and will route it when they land. Without approval only when the step is `not_required`, the agent is in `act` and every planned channel's connection allows `create` on `auto` (invariant 8). A rejected plan ends as `declined` (nothing sent). |
| Attempts | Each attempt is its own external action, key `<run>:<node>:reach:<n>`. It is proposed as approved under the plan (`runPlannedAction` in packages/agents) and executed once. The connector's own checks (consent, opt-out, quiet hours, A2P) still run, and a matrix set to `off` since approval refuses the attempt. A failed channel (no consent now, refused, provider error) is recorded and the next one tried at once. The voice attempt's purpose is the step's fixed purpose. |
| Waiting | The executor gains a `waiting` outcome and the port methods `waitDone` and `afterWait`. The run checks before waiting, waits for `reachout-<id>` or the timeout, and then reads the `reach_out` row, which is the truth (the event only wakes the run). The cron (`reachOutTick`, every minute per client) ends overdue reach-outs as `no_reply`, sends each next attempt once `next_attempt_at` is due, and re-sends the event of an ended reach-out until the run takes it, for a day, like inbox resolutions (§1.5). |
| Replies | Matched as events are dispatched (before routing). A `message.received` from the contact ends their most recently updated open reach-out as `replied`, with the message's text from its raw record as untrusted data. STOP ends every open one to them as `opted_out`, and START and HELP are ignored. A `call.completed` with outcome `answered` ends the reach-out whose attempt placed that call, or for an inbound call the latest open one. Email replies come to the client's forwarding address with `+<reach-out id>`: the Worker's `email()` matches the tag and the client's stored address, accepts only the contact's own address (the envelope sender, or the From header when DMARC passed), and keeps the reply's own words (quoted history cut, at most 4000 characters).
