Anyone else writing to the tagged address is ignored. |
| Output | `outcome`, `channel` (the reply's, else the last sent), `reply` (`{channel, text, untrusted: true, rawRecordId, at}`), `contact`, `reason`, `attempts` (sent), `sources` (`about`) and `reachOutId`. A reach-out that expects no reply ends at its first sent attempt as `no_reply`. A test run sends nothing and returns `outcome: no_reply` with `testRun: true` and the plan. |
| Language | The contact's, else the client's (`tenant.settings.language`), else English. A fixed template picks that language's text, else English (English is required). Composed messages are written by the agent's model from the fixed purpose, with the record's data passed as quoted data. Its cost goes on the step. |
| Evidence | Each attempt, refusal, timeout and reply is a line in the contact's thread. Its body carries `reachOut: {id, runId, agentVersionId, sources}` and the message row carries `agent_version_id` and `sources` (invariant 10). The connectors still write their own lines (the text, the call, the email). |
| Email (`resend`) | Mezonas' infrastructure, set up by the operator: `/api/ops/clients/:id/email` adds the client's domain at Resend and shows its DNS records. Until the domain is verified, email goes from our fallback domain (`RESEND_FALLBACK_DOMAIN`). The connection holds a sending-only key restricted to the domain it sends from, never Mezonas' key, and verifying swaps in a key for the client's domain. `email.send` is behind approval like every send. It is sent once per key (Resend's `Idempotency-Key` header, plus our own record by key), plain text only, from `<localPart>@<domain>`, with the reply-to tagged. `email.send` is the provider-neutral email channel tool. Gmail's send (an agent's own mailbox or a person's) will supply the same shape behind its own binding when M10 lands. |
| Screens | Contacts (both surfaces) gain email, preferred channels in order, language, timezone, quiet hours, roles and notes, and email consent. A client agent's page gains a People tab: roles → contacts and conditions (channels, urgent outside hours, per day, escalate after, only if) beside the operator's Channels section. The portal gains People for the client admin (no channels). The messaging page gains an Email panel with the domain's DNS records. The builder's inspector has a reach-out form: role, purpose, about, urgency, wait for a reply, expect a reply, and the message as fixed text per language or composed. Portal strings are plain English for the languages stream. |
| Not built | Gmail send (M10). Meta's WhatsApp. Bounces and complaints from Resend (they don't opt anyone out yet). Revoking a client's sending key on disconnect. Approver rules routing. Translation of replies (§1.18's next step). Counting emails into `usage_daily` for credits. The "only if" editor offers one rule row (the stored rules take any number). |

---

### 1.26 Access: the vault, grants, the code service and browser logins (owner, 2026-11-05; design)

The owner wants agents to sign in to websites and work on their own, with access segregated: a vault per
client, second factors kept apart, and each agent given only the tools it needs (ARCHITECTURE v0.10,
§6.9, §6.10). Built in three phases (M12), each its own PRs; migrations first, in their own PRs.

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Vault = connections | A vault item is a `connection` row: OAuth connections, API-key connections (`auth: 'api_key'`) and browser logins (connector `browser`). No second store: bindings, the permission matrix, status, the portal's Connections page and disconnect already work on connections. // DECISION |
| Client data keys (phase 1, migration 0011) | `tenant_key` (tenant table, RLS forced): `id` (uuid v7, the key id), `tenant_id`, `wrapped_key` (the 32-byte data key, AES-256-GCM under `ENCRYPTION_KEY`, additional data = the client id), `status` (`active`, `retired`, `destroyed`), `created_at`, `retired_at`. One `active` key per client, created on first use. Envelope v2 `{v: 2, alg, kid: tenant_key.id, iv, ct}` with the same binding to client and connection as v1; v1 envelopes still decrypt; new writes are always v2; the cron re-encrypts a bounded batch of v1 rows each hour until none are left. Master key rotation re-wraps `tenant_key` rows (`CredentialCipher.rotate` gains a rewrap). Destroying a client's key is an operator action only on a retired client; it wipes `wrapped_key` and sets `destroyed`. |
| Write-only secrets | No route returns a secret; read models say only what is set (`{username: "agent@client.com", password: "set", updatedAt}`; usernames are not secret). API-key connectors declare their fields in the manifest (`credentialFields`: name, label, secret or not); the portal shows a form for them. The client admin enters secrets; the operator's screens never offer the field. |
| Grants (phase 1, migration 0011) | `agent_grant` (tenant table): `tenant_id`, `agent_id`, `connection_id`, `limits` (`{verbs?: {read?, create?, update?, delete?: 'auto' \| 'approval' \| 'off'}, tools?: string[]}`), `updated_by`, `updated_at`; unique (`agent_id`, `connection_id`). The binding is the grant; a row only narrows it, and no row means no narrowing, so today's agents keep working unchanged. Effective verb = the strictest of the connection's matrix and the grant's verb; then the step's approval and the agent's mode as today (`decideToolCall` takes the grant). A tool outside `limits.tools` is refused like an unbound connector. Operator: set any limit up to the matrix. Client admin: only tighten, and revoke (verbs all `off`). Each change is an `agent_change` row (`kind: 'grant'`, before and after). |
| Access page (portal) | `/access` for the client admin: every vault item, what it is (connection, API key, browser login, with the site's tier), whether its secret is set, and each agent that can use it with what; tighten or revoke per agent; disconnect. The back office shows the same on the client page and the agent page, where the operator binds and sets grants. |
| The code service (phase 2) | `apps/mfa`, a Worker deployed separately (`mezonas-codes`), with `MFA_ENCRYPTION_KEY` (generated once by the deploy job, like `ENCRYPTION_KEY`), a Durable Object per identity (SQLite storage), and no access to Neon, R2 or `apps/api`'s secrets. RPC entry point (service binding `CODES` in `apps/api`, a `WorkerEntrypoint`): `createIdentity({tenantId, label})`, `identity(id)`, `setAuthenticator({identityId, siteKey, otpauthUri})`, `openWait({identityId, siteKey, channel: 'sms' \| 'email' \| 'totp', sender, pattern, ttlSeconds ≤ 300})`, `claim(waitId)` → `{status: 'code', code} \| 'pending' \| 'expired'`, `alerts({since})`, `deleteIdentity(id)`. Every call carries the tenant id and is refused for an identity of another client. Public routes: Twilio's inbound SMS webhook (signature checked with the codes subaccount's token) and the `email()` handler for `AGENT_EMAIL_DOMAIN` (postal-mime; SPF, DKIM and DMARC must pass). Messages live only until claimed or 10 minutes; logs keep metadata, never codes. Cap: 20 code requests per identity per day (setting). An unsolicited code or a reset-looking message becomes an alert; `apps/api`'s cron reads `alerts` and raises inbox items for the operator and the client admin. |
| Agent identities | Email `agent-<short id>@<AGENT_EMAIL_DOMAIN>`; a phone number only when a site sends codes by text, bought by the code service in its own Twilio subaccount (`MFA_TWILIO_*`), separate from clients' messaging subaccounts. A browser connection's config names its identity (`identityId`, not secret). |
| Browser sites (phase 3, migration 0012) | `browser_site` and `browser_site_version` (operator tables, `tenant_id` null, visible only to operator sessions, versions immutable like skills): `slug`, `name`, `tier` (`low`, `medium`, `high`, `blocked`), `login` (`url`, `origins`, selectors for username, password and submit, `secondFactor`: `none`, `totp`, `sms` or `email` with `sender` and `pattern`, `signedIn` check by URL pattern or selector), `signOut`, `allow` and `block` URL patterns (the platform's blocked defaults always apply), `notes` (terms checked, by whom). A client's browser connection names the site and pins the version it was set up on; the client can make it stricter (sign-in by a person only, or no writes), never looser. |
| Binding browser sites | Bindings are keyed by connector; browser connections bind by site: the key is `browser:<site slug>`, and browser tools take `site` in their input. Connector manifests gain an optional `bindingKey(input)`; the runtime uses it when present (no special case for `browser` in `packages/agents`). // DECISION |
| Browser driver | `packages/connectors/src/browser/` talks to a `BrowserDriver` interface (open page, go to, fill, click, read text, read tables, download, cookies, close). `apps/api` implements it on the Browser Run binding (`BROWSER`, `@cloudflare/playwright`); tests implement it on local Playwright against fixture pages served by the test. Sign-in, sign-out and URL checks are code in the connector; the model gets only `browser.page.read` (text and tables of an allowed URL, password fields removed), `browser.file.download` (into documents, as uploads are) and, after the pilot, `browser.form.submit` (external, behind approval, the page's fields before as its before state). |
| Sessions and stops | One browser per step, closed at its end. Low tier: the session's cookies may be saved encrypted in the connection's credentials and reused until they fail. Two failed sign-ins set the connection to `needs_attention` and stop further sign-ins; a CAPTCHA, an unexpected page or a new-device check fails the step with a reason and raises an inbox item. Caps: sign-ins per connection per day (default 10) and optional hours, checked in code. Screenshots after sign-in only, kept with the step as evidence. High-tier sites: no autonomous sign-in; a person signs in through Browser Run's human handoff, which comes after the pilot. |
| Human release (phase 4, owner 2026-11-05; migration 0013) | Optional per connection, off by default: `connection.config.release = {mode: 'off' \| 'code', personId (a user of the client with a verified phone or email), channel: 'sms' \| 'email', window: 'run' \| {hours: 1–24}}`. Table `access_release` (tenant table, RLS forced): `id`, `tenant_id`, `connection_id`, `agent_id`, `run_id`, `person_id`, `channel`, `code_hash` (HMAC-SHA256 under a key derived from `ENCRYPTION_KEY`, never the code), `expires_at` (10 minutes), `tries` (max 5), `status` (`pending`, `released`, `expired`, `failed`, `revoked`, `void`), `released_at`, `valid_until`, `revoked_by`; append-mostly (status columns only). The gate (`callTool`) checks for a valid release before any call through a connection with `mode: 'code'`; without one the step creates a request (voiding older pending ones), sends the code (text through the client's Twilio messaging when it has a number, else email through Mezonas' sending domain), raises an inbox item for that person and waits on it like an approval; entering the code in the portal or replying to the message from the person's own number or address resolves it. Window `run`: valid for that run only; hours: valid for that connection and agent until `valid_until`. Wait timeout: the code's 10 minutes, re-sendable up to 3 times by the person; then the step fails `access_not_released`. The model never sees a code; inbound messages carrying one are consumed by the release check and never reach an agent's thread. Client admin: turn on or off, pick the person and window, revoke live releases (Access page). Operator: turn on only. Test runs need a release too. |
| Pilot | A fixture supplier portal in tests (low tier, emailed codes); then one real low-tier site the owner names, read and download only. |

#### Decisions made while building phase 1 (2026-11-05)

Built on `claude/access-keys-migration` (migration 0011, #44) and `claude/access-vault-grants`.

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| `tenant_key` | `wrapped_key` is jsonb shaped like the v1 envelope (`{v, alg, kid, iv, ct}`, `kid` = the master key's id), so a rotation finds the rows to re-wrap; null once destroyed (a check keeps `destroyed` and null together). A partial unique index keeps one `active` key per client, so two first writes at once end with one key. No `DELETE` grant. // DECISION |
| Envelope v2 | Additional data: the label `countersign/credentials/v2`, the client id and the connection id; the key is looked up by `kid` within the binding's client, so a ciphertext copied to another client finds no key and one copied to another connection fails to decrypt. A data key is wrapped with the label `countersign/tenant-key/v1` and the client id as additional data. The master cipher no longer writes credentials: `encryptV1` is for tests and fixtures; the platform writes through `VaultCipher` (`CredentialCipher.forClients`), in routes over the request's transaction and in runs one short `withTenant` per read or write. // DECISION |
| First use and destroy | The client's key is created on its first write. A client whose key was destroyed gets no new key (writes fail with `key_destroyed`). `destroyClientKeys` wipes every wrapped key and also drops the client's remaining v1 envelopes, so nothing of the client stays readable. There is no "retired client" state yet, so the store function is built and tested and the operator route is left out (open item below). // DECISION |
| Hourly upkeep | At the usage sweep minute the cron re-wraps keys still wrapped by a previous master key and re-encrypts up to 200 v1 envelopes across clients, each client in its own transaction; rows that fail to decrypt are counted, logged and left; a failure never holds up the rest of the cron. Rotation steps are in `docs/operations.md`. // DECISION |
| Write-only form | The client admin enters every field each time (secrets are never merged with stored values, so no route decrypts to edit). The read model is `connection.config.vault` (which fields are set, non-secret values, when), so no read decrypts anything; disconnecting drops it with the credentials; `connection_change` names the fields, never values. An API-key connector with `credentialFields` shows on Connections with a link to Access instead of "Not available yet". // DECISION |
| No secret in any response | Core's contract refuses response fields named like a secret (`password`, `apiKey`, `accessToken`, `credentials`, `wrappedKey`, …) on every route, and the API test looks for a stored password in every response either side can read. The webhook setup's `signingSecret` (Mezonas' own signing key, shown to the client who pastes it into Zapier) predates this and is left as it is (open item). // DECISION |
| Grant checks | A grant row that no longer parses narrows to everything off (fails closed). The client's loosening check compares grant levels (a verb left out is `auto`) and tool subsets, so it refuses `loosening` (403) even where the matrix would still hold; the operator's limits are checked against the matrix when set (422 `beyond_matrix`), and a later tightening of the matrix still wins at the call. Tools in a grant must be the connector's. An approved action is not re-checked at execution, as for the matrix. // DECISION |
| Where grants apply | Everywhere an agent acts through a connection: the gate (`callTool`), reach-out attempts (`planned.ts`, on the agent's channel connection) and inbound call answering (`calls/answer.ts`). // DECISION |
| `bindingKey` | A code hook on `Connector` (not in the Zod manifest). Its key must be the connector id or start with `<connector id>:`; null, a throw or another connector's key refuses the call like an unbound connector. Bindings accept `<connector>:<name>` keys for a connection of that connector. // DECISION |
| Access page | `/access` for every client role (read-only for client users, as Connections), with each item's kind from the connector's auth (`oauth`, `api_key`, `built_in`; `browser` and the site's tier come in phase 3), the agents' effective levels (the stricter of matrix and grant) and tool subsets; tighten offers only the current level or stricter. The back office's grants are on the client agent's Operation tab, under the bindings. // DECISION |

Open after phase 1: a "retired client" state and the operator route that destroys its key; whether the webhook setup should stop showing its signing secret; the vault on the back office's client page; a fluent speaker's review of the new portal strings; a walk in Chromium.

#### Decisions made while building phase 2 (the code service, 2026-11-05)

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Identity ids | 12 lowercase base32 characters (60 random bits). The id is the `Identity` object's name and the address's local part (`agent-<id>@<AGENT_EMAIL_DOMAIN>`), so inbound mail needs no lookup table. Not a secret. // DECISION |
| A second object class | `Directory`, one per client (named by the client id), lists that client's identity ids so `alerts({tenantId, since})` can read each identity's alerts. Alerts stay with their identity. Binding `DIRECTORY` beside `IDENTITY`. // DECISION |
| RPC shape | Every method takes one object with `tenantId` (and `identityId` where it applies) and answers `{ok: true, value}` or `{ok: false, error: {code, message}}`; codes `invalid`, `not_found` (also for another client's identity, so existence isn't revealed), `daily_cap`, `conflict`, `not_configured`, `unavailable`. `claim` takes `identityId` with the `waitId`. Schemas and the `CodesService` interface are in core (`packages/core/src/codes/service.ts`). The extra method `addPhone({tenantId, identityId, areaCode?})` buys a number. // DECISION |
| Texts | Each number's `SmsUrl` is `MFA_ORIGIN/twilio/sms/<identity id>`; the signature covers that URL, and the text must be to the identity's own number and from the codes subaccount (`AccountSid`). US local numbers only. Deleting an identity releases its number. // DECISION |
| Email authentication | Only the topmost `Authentication-Results` or `ARC-Authentication-Results` header is read, and only when its authserv-id is `mx.cloudflare.net`; SPF, DKIM and DMARC must all pass and DMARC's `header.from` must be the From domain. A wait's email sender is an address or `@domain` (that domain and its subdomains). Unauthenticated email is ignored without an alert. To verify against Cloudflare: `apps/mfa/NOTES.md`. // DECISION |
| Email codes off until verified | Var `EMAIL_CODES`, `off` in `apps/mfa/wrangler.jsonc` for every environment. While off, `openWait({channel: 'email'})` answers `not_configured` ("email codes are off until verified") and `email()` still reads messages, but never fills a wait from one; it raises alerts as for any unmatched message. Reason: if Cloudflare does not prepend its own authentication header, a sender-written `mx.cloudflare.net; spf=pass; dkim=pass; dmarc=pass` header would be read as authentic. It is switched `on` per environment only after a real forged message is shown to be rejected (`apps/mfa/NOTES.md`, "Turning email codes on"). Texts and authenticator codes are not affected. (Coordinator, 2026-11-05.) // DECISION |
| Reading codes | The wait's pattern runs over the subject and text with links removed (a sign-in link's token is never a code); its first capture group, or the whole match, is the code (at most 32 characters). The first open wait on the channel whose sender and pattern both match takes it. // DECISION |
| Alerts | A message no wait takes becomes `unsolicited_code`, `reset_attempt` or (a third kind) `unexpected_message`, keeping the channel, the sender and the times only. A code that arrives after its wait expired is an alert too. Kept 30 days. // DECISION |
| Retention | Waits last at most 300 seconds (minimum 30, default 300); a held code is deleted when claimed, by an alarm ten minutes after it arrived, and a claim after the wait's time answers `expired` even if a code came. The daily cap counts opened waits (`MFA_DAILY_CAP`, default 20, per UTC day). // DECISION |
| Sealing | AES-256-GCM under `MFA_ENCRYPTION_KEY`; additional data names the identity, the site and what it is (`authenticator`, or `code:<wait id>`). Held codes are sealed too. Envelopes have no key id yet: rotation is not built (`apps/mfa/NOTES.md`). // DECISION |
| Authenticators | `otpauth://totp/` only; SHA1, SHA256 or SHA512; 6 to 8 digits; period 15 to 120 seconds. The code is computed at claim time. // DECISION |
| Inbox items | apps/api's cron reads each client's alerts (when `CODES` is bound) and makes two `review` items per alert, one `assignee_role: 'operator'` and one `'client_admin'`, with `subject_type: 'code_alert'` and the alert's id as subject; no migration. Each read starts five minutes before the newest alert already raised (30 days back the first time) and skips alerts that already have their items. // DECISION |
| Layering | `scripts/check-deps.mjs`: `apps/mfa` may depend on `@countersign/core` only, its runtime dependencies are a fixed list (core, hono, postal-mime, zod), and no package may depend on `@countersign/mfa`. Tested by `scripts/check-deps.test.mjs`, run with `pnpm lint:deps`. // DECISION |
| Domains | Placeholders until the owner confirms: `codes-staging.mezonas.com` and `codes.mezonas.com` (Twilio's webhooks; one level under the zone, so the zone's certificate covers them), `agents.staging.mezonas.com` and `agents.mezonas.com` (agent email, like `in.staging.mezonas.com` for forwarding). |

#### Decisions made while building phase 3 (browser connections, 2026-11-05)

Built on `claude/browser-sites-migration` (migration 0012) and `claude/browser-connections`; the `browser` binding and CI's Chromium are their own PRs.

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Operator tables | `browser_site` and `browser_site_version` have no `tenant_id` column: no client ever owns a site, and RLS (forced) is the templates' `tenant_id IS NULL AND app_is_operator()` reduced to `app_is_operator()` for every command. The Worker inserts and updates sites and only inserts versions; nothing is deleted; versions are immutable by trigger. // DECISION |
| Live tier, pinned profile | The tier, name and notes live on `browser_site` and change in place (setting the tier needs a note and logs a `browser_tier` line on each client login it reaches), so a site moved to `blocked` stops every connection at once. The login steps and URL rules are versioned; a connection pins the version it was set up on (`connection.config.siteVersionId`, no foreign key; versions are never deleted). A new version needs a change (`no_op` otherwise); moving connections to a newer version is not built. // DECISION |
| Reading the catalogue | Runs read their pinned version and the live tier in an operator transaction that names no client (`withOperator(db, [])`, apps/api `pinnedSite`), so no client row is visible beside it. Portal requests hold the one pooled connection in their transaction, so they switch scope inside it for the read only (`readOperatorCatalogue` in packages/db: `app.tenant_ids` empty and `app.operator` on, both put back after). The portal sees slug, name, tier and the second factor's kind, never a profile. // DECISION |
| Profile | Core's `BrowserSiteProfile`: `login` (`url`, `origins` (exact, https; plain http only for loopback, for the tests' fixture sites), `usernameField`, `passwordField`, `submitButton` (named so, as the contract refuses response fields named `password`), `secondFactor` (`none`, `totp`, `sms` or `email` with `sender` and `pattern`, plus `codeField` and `codeButton`), `signedIn` (URL pattern and/or selector), `stopSelectors`), `signOut` (URL and/or selector, on a login origin), `allow` (patterns that name their origin outright), `block`, `hosts` (extra hosts pages load from), `session.keep`, `notes`. Patterns: `*` is any run of characters, case-insensitive; `/…` or `*…` match the path and query, others the whole address; checked both as written and percent-decoded. // DECISION |
| Always blocked | `*setting*`, `*password*`, `*passwd*`, `*/users*`, `*/user-management*`, `*permission*`, `*/roles*`, `*billing*`, `*payment*`, `*delete*` on every site, whatever its profile says; a profile cannot unblock them. // DECISION |
| Tier rules | `blocked`: never signs in (refused before a browser opens). `high` (or a client's "a person signs in"): refused with "a person must sign in … not available yet" until the human handoff exists. `low` and `medium` sign in on their own. Writes (none exist yet): `browserWriteCeiling` gives `off` (blocked, or the client's "no changes"), `approval` (medium and high) or `matrix` (low). Only low tier with `session.keep` keeps a session. // DECISION |
| One browser per tool call | Each `browser.page.read` or `browser.file.download` opens a fresh browser, signs in (or restores a kept session), does its one thing, then signs out (or keeps the session) and closes, in a `finally`. Tool calls have no step-end hook to close a browser kept across a step's calls; each call that signs in counts toward the daily cap. The plan's "one browser per step" waits for such a hook. // DECISION |
| Sign-in by code | Go to the login page; check stops; refuse to type unless the page is on a login origin (checked again before each field and the code); open the code wait before submitting; fill and submit; if not signed in yet and a code field is there, claim with a bounded poll (default every 2 s, 45 times; the wait lasts 180 s); fill the code; check the signed-in marker. Errors during sign-in are replaced by our own messages, so nothing typed can leak through a driver's error. A site that signs in without asking for the code is fine (the wait expires). // DECISION |
| Stops and failures | A CAPTCHA (common widgets and the profile's `stopSelectors`), an unusual-activity, new-device or locked-account page, or an unexpected page (a sign-in that moves off the login origins) is a stop: the step fails with the reason, the connection goes to `error` (the existing "needs attention"; no new status) with `config.browserState.stopped`, a `browser_stopped` log line, and `review` inbox items for the operator and the client admin (`subject_type: 'browser_stop'`). Wrong login or no code in time counts a failure; two in a row stop the same way. Entering the login again on Access clears the stop and the failures (the person has checked the account); today's count stays. // DECISION |
| Caps | `connection.config.caps`: `signInsPerDay` (default 10, per UTC day, counted before each fresh sign-in) and optional `hours` (`start`, `end`, `timezone`; overnight windows wrap), checked in code before a browser opens. Counters in `connection.config.browserState`. Not editable on a screen yet. // DECISION |
| Kept sessions | Low tier with `session.keep` only: after a clean call the cookies are saved with the login in the connection's credentials (the vault, the client's key) and the browser closes without signing out; the next call restores them and checks the signed-in marker on the login page, else forgets them and signs in afresh. A failed call signs out and forgets them; medium tier never saves one and drops any left. Re-entering the login drops it too. // DECISION |
| What the model sees | Tool results are page data only: the URL, text and tables (`readText` removes password and hidden inputs, one-time-code and password-autocomplete fields, scripts and hidden elements; other fields read as their values), or the document's id and file facts. Every string returned, and every error, has the password, cookie values (8+ characters) and the code replaced by `[hidden]`. Responses may never carry `cookies` (core's contract). // DECISION |
| Evidence | A screenshot after sign-in and after each page opened, stored in object storage beside documents (`kind: browser_evidence`); never during sign-in. Each call's session (sign-in kind, pages, actions, screenshot keys, outcome, signed out or kept) is a `browser_session` line in `connection_change`. Session recording on Browser Run is off. The run timeline's plain summary of a session is not built. // DECISION |
| Browser Run | `@cloudflare/playwright` `launch(env.BROWSER, {recording: false, guardrails: {allowedDomains: the site's hosts, allowedDomainSets: ['common-cdns']}})`, one context and page, `acceptDownloads: false` (downloads are fetched in the page with its cookies, same origin). The connector talks to a structural Playwright page (`playwrightTab`), so local Chromium runs the same adapter in tests. The `BROWSER` binding is optional in the env type; the connector shows "not available yet" without it. // DECISION |
| Downloads | `browser.file.download` fetches an allowed URL in the page, re-checks the final address after redirects, accepts PDFs and images only, and stores the file with `storeDocument` (`source: 'connector'`, `meta: {via: 'browser', site, sourceUrl}`); `document.received` goes out through the outbox. It is a read (`sideEffect: 'none'`), so it also becomes a tool-mode skill. // DECISION |
| Setup | The operator offers the `browser` connector to a client; the client admin then sees every non-blocked site on Access and sets a login up after confirming they own the account and the site's terms allow automated access (a portal check, not stored). Setup pins the current version and, for sites with a second factor, makes an identity at the code service (`createIdentity`; `addPhone` for texts; `setAuthenticator` with the pasted `otpauth://` link for authenticator sites, never stored in apps/api). One login per site per client. The login itself goes through `PUT /api/connections/browser/credentials` with the new optional `connectionId` (required for browser). The generic API-key setup form leaves `browser` out. // DECISION |
| Stricter only | `PUT /api/access/browser/:connectionId` takes `{noWrites, personSignIn}`; turning either off is 403 `loosening`. Loosening a client's setting is not built for the operator yet. // DECISION |
| Bindings in the back office | The agent's Operation panel lists a `<connector>:<site>` row for each of the client's connections that carry a site, so website logins bind as `browser:<slug>`. // DECISION |
| Real browser in tests | apps/api has `playwright` (1.63.0) as a dev dependency; `src/browser/chromium.test.ts` serves a fixture supplier portal on 127.0.0.1 and runs the connector in Chromium. CI installs Chromium with apps/api's own Playwright (its own PR). // DECISION |

Open after phase 3: the run timeline's summary of a browser session; moving client logins to a newer site version; the operator loosening a client's stricter setting; editing caps and hours on a screen; the portal's Connections page shows one card for all website logins (the first one's status) and points to Access; the high tier's human handoff; `browser.form.submit` after the pilot; emailed codes stay off until verified (phase 2), so the first real site should use texts, an authenticator or no second factor, or wait for that check.

#### Decisions made while building phase 4 (2026-11-05)

Human release (ARCHITECTURE §6.9 "Human release"; the row "Human release" above). Built on `claude/access-release-migration` (migration 0013, `access_release`, chained after phase 3's 0012) and `claude/access-release`.

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| `access_release` | The plan's columns plus `created_at`. Composite foreign keys keep the connection, agent and run in one client; checks on status, channel, `tries` (0–5) and `released_at` (set exactly for released and revoked rows). A trigger lets only `status`, `tries`, `released_at`, `valid_until` and `revoked_by` change and refuses DELETE; no DELETE grant. `channel` is the channel the code actually went by. // DECISION |
| The person's phone and email | Users hold only their sign-in email (verified by WorkOS), so email codes go there. A text goes to the client's contact with the same email address, only with a phone, text consent and no STOP. The number is pinned in `config.release.phone` when the setting is saved (by the client admin, or by the operator turning it on); if the contact's phone changes later, codes go by email until the setting is saved again, so editing a contact never redirects codes. Saving text for someone no text can reach is refused (422 `no_phone`). // DECISION |
| Consent and quiet hours | A release text is a transactional message to the client's own person, but it honours what the messaging code enforces: text consent, STOP, A2P 10DLC for US numbers, the client's and the person's quiet hours. Where any of them stops a text (or the client has no number), the code goes by email from Mezonas' fallback domain instead, which has no quiet hours, so a release is never blocked by them. // DECISION |
| The code | Six digits by rejection sampling from `crypto.getRandomValues`. Stored as hex HMAC-SHA256 of `<request id>:<code>` under a key HMAC-derived from `ENCRYPTION_KEY` with the label `countersign/access-release/v1` (never the raw key); checked in constant time under the current and previous keys (`ReleaseCodeKeys`, packages/connectors). 10 minutes, 5 tries, one use. Never logged, never in a step output, tool result, event, inbox payload or response; core's contract refuses response fields named `codeHash` or `releaseCode`. // DECISION |
| Sending | Texts from the client's number through its subaccount, not the `sms.send` tool: nothing is recorded (no raw record of the body, no thread line, no status callback). Emails from `Mezonas <codes@RESEND_FALLBACK_DOMAIN>` with Mezonas' key, subject "Your Mezonas code", idempotency key `release-<id>`, reply-to the client's forwarding address with the tag `+rel<request id>` when it has one. No link in either. Sending happens inside the request's transaction, so a failed send leaves no request; the step then fails with the reason. // DECISION |
| The message's language | The text, the email (subject and last line) and the inbox item's title are in the person's language: their own choice (`user_languages`), else the client's default, else English. The six languages' words are core's `RELEASE_WORDS` (beside the timeline words, §1.18): the Worker writes them, and the portal's message files live in the web bundle, which apps/api doesn't import. Twilio's read tools hide a code text in any of the six languages. // DECISION |
| The gate | `callTool` checks the release after the binding, the grant and the matrix decision, before the connector runs, through a `ReleasePort` (`covers`, `request`) that apps/api implements; `packages/agents` imports no database for it. A setting that no longer parses or names nobody refuses the call (fails closed). Test runs need a release too. Only the gate ever asks for a release. // DECISION |
| Where releases apply | Every path where an agent uses a connection checks the same thing (`releaseCoversTx`: a live release for that agent and connection under the setting as it is now), and only the gate asks for one; everywhere else, no live release means no: (1) the gate (`callTool`) asks and waits; (2) an approved write (`executeAction`, after an approval or on a retry) runs only under a live release for its agent and run, else the action fails before it is claimed: an approval is not a release; (3) reach-out attempts (`planned.ts`) are refused; (4) a voice agent's tools during a call (`/api/voice/:tenant/tools/:name`) get a port that covers but never asks, so they are refused ("can't be asked for on a call"); (5) inbound call answering (`planInbound`) answers with an agent only under a live hours release (there is no run yet, so a run release never covers it), else the call is forwarded or goes to voicemail, and the setup preview says why. No path asks mid-call or goes on without one. Website logins (phase 3) are vault items like any other: `browser.page.read` and `browser.file.download` open a browser, sign in or reuse a kept session only inside the tool, behind the gate, so a login with `release.mode = 'code'` signs in only under a live release; no other path opens a browser (the setup and "stricter" routes don't), and a call's tools refuse them as above. The Access page shows the setting for website logins too. Tests: the gate's unit tests, `executeAction` and `runPlannedAction` unit tests, an API test of an approved write after a revoke, and the call tests for a voice tool and for inbound answering. // DECISION |
| Waiting | The step ends its attempt at the gate (`awaiting_release`; the model never learns of it) and waits on the person's inbox item event with the code's time left as its timeout (the executor's `waiting` outcome, `reason: "release"`, "waiting for a code" on the timeline). Released: the step runs again from the start with the input on its `step_run` row, as a retried step does (writes it proposed are found by their keys). A newer code with time left: it waits again. Otherwise it fails `access_not_released`, the request is marked expired and the item closed. The item is resolved when released, so the cron's redelivery of resolutions covers a lost event. // DECISION |
| The inbox item | Kind `request`, assigned to the person (`assignee_user_id`), subject `access_release` with the current request's id (moved on a re-send), title "Enter the code we texted (emailed) you to let {agent} use {tool}". Its payload names the connection, tool, agent and channel, never the code or where it went. The generic resolve routes refuse it (409 `enter_code`): it is resolved only by its code. No new inbox kind, so 0013 touches no other table. // DECISION |
| Requests, re-sends and voids | A new request for the same connection, agent and run voids the older pending one and closes its item. The person may ask for a new code 3 times while the step waits (the count is in the item's payload); each voids the current one and keeps the item. A request whose 5 tries are used is `failed`; a new code can still be asked for while the step waits. // DECISION |
| Windows | `run`: `valid_until` is null and the release covers that run only. Hours: `valid_until` = release time + hours, any run of that connection and agent. A release counts only under the setting as it is now: the same person, the window no longer than the setting's, and given after the setting was last saved, so turning it off and on, naming someone else or shortening the window tightens at once. // DECISION |
| Replies by text | An inbound text that is only a code (spaces and dashes allowed) is checked before anything is stored: from the pinned phone of a person with a pending text request it is a try (released or a wrong try); the right code from any other number is consumed but releases nothing and uses no try (anyone could burn tries), logged as `wrong_sender`. A consumed text is kept only as a `ReleaseReply` raw record without its text, so Twilio's retries do nothing; it never reaches a contact's thread or an event. The Twilio read tools show a release text, and any message that is only six digits, as hidden. // DECISION |
| Replies by email | Accepted only to `<forwarding address>+rel<request id>`, from the person's sign-in address in the From header with DMARC passing for its domain (the inbound path's Authentication-Results check, which needs SPF or DKIM aligned); the envelope sender alone never counts. The reply's new text must be the code alone, so an auto-reply or a quoting forward can't release. Anything else to that address is dropped and logged (`email_ignored`); it is never forwarded mail. // DECISION |
| Who does what | Client admin: turn on or off, the person (a client admin or user of the client), the channel, the window; revoke live releases. The operator: turn on only, while it is off (403 `client_only` to turn it off or change it). Only the person the code went to enters it or asks for a new one (403 `not_yours`). Every setting change, request, release, failed try, failure, expiry, re-send, void, revoke and ignored reply is a `connection_change` row (`kind: "release"`), with ids, never a code. // DECISION |
| Screens | The portal's Access page shows each vault item's human release, its live releases with Revoke, and the form; the inbox item shows the code entry (one-time-code field, tries left, a new code) for its person only, with no approve or answer buttons. The back office shows the setting on the agent's grants, read-only, with "Turn on". New portal strings in all six languages. // DECISION |

Open after phase 4: a fluent speaker's review of the new strings; a walk in Chromium; whether a re-send should be possible after the step has stopped waiting (it isn't).

### 1.27 The directory (owner, 2026-11-05; design)

One directory per client of the people and organizations its agents work with, shared by every agent of that client (invariant 1: never across clients). Builds on contacts (§1.22, §1.25) and the `vendor` record (§1.6).

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Data (migration 0014, own PR) | New tenant table `organization`: `id`, `tenant_id`, `name`, `kinds` (`vendor`, `customer`, `partner`, `own`, `other`; several allowed), `website`, `email`, `phone`, `address` (json), `notes`, `external_refs` (ids in connected tools), `vendor_id` (unique, links a vendor organization to its `vendor` record so there is one entry), `source` (`manual`, `csv`, `<connector>`, `agent`), `created_by`, timestamps; RLS forced. `contact` gains nullable `organization_id`, `title`, `relationship` (`employee`, `contractor`, `customer`, `vendor`, `partner`, `other`) and `source`. Additive. Changes are logged (`contact_change`, and `organization_change` alike). |
| Who edits | The client admin and the operator, fully (as contacts today); client users read. CSV import (people or organizations, with a preview and a per-row result). Sync from connected tools as the connectors allow (Zoho CRM and Podio contacts now; QuickBooks vendors, customers and employees with M9), always as suggestions the first time, then kept in step by `external_refs`. |
| Agents | Tools `directory.search` (name, email, phone, organization, relationship, role; read), `directory.get` (read) and `directory.suggest` (internal: an addition or change the client admin accepts or declines in the inbox). A suggested change to an email or phone never applies without acceptance, since it changes where messages go; consent and opt-outs stay as §1.22. Directory text reaches models only in tool results (invariant 9). Built-in skill `directory.lookup`. Reach out (§1.25) picks people from the directory. |
| Screens | Portal `/directory`: people and organizations, search and filters, edit, import, pending suggestions. Back office: the same on the client page. All portal strings in six languages. |

#### Decisions made while building the directory (2026-11-05)

Built on `claude/directory` over migration 0014 (`claude/directory-migration`, its own PR).

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Data (migration 0014) | As designed, plus `contact.external_refs` (jsonb, default `{}`), since synced people are kept in step by their ids in the tool and contacts had nowhere to keep them. `organization.kinds` is a `text[]` checked against the list; `vendor_id` is unique per client with a composite foreign key, so a vendor has one organization of the same client. `contact.organization_id` has a composite foreign key too. `source` is a slug (`manual`, `csv`, `agent`, a connector id); contacts made before the directory keep it null. Organizations are never deleted, like contacts. `contact_reachable_check` stays: a person needs an email, a phone or a WhatsApp number. |
| People are contacts | The directory's people are the client's contacts. Their new fields (organization, title, relationship) go through the contact routes and `contact_change`, so consent, opt-outs and reach out are unchanged; the directory screen edits name, email, phone, title, relationship and organization, and Contacts keeps consent. |
| One name per organization | A client can't have two organizations with the same name (case aside), so imports and suggestions find the one there is (`organization_exists`, 409). |
| Vendor link | "Link to its vendor record" runs `findOrCreateVendor` on the organization's name (normalized name or alias, made when missing) and adds the `vendor` kind; a vendor already linked to another organization is refused (`vendor_linked`). Unlinking leaves the vendor record. A linked organization keeps the `vendor` kind. On create and import (coordinator, 2026-11-05), a vendor organization links itself when a vendor record's normalized name is exactly its own and no other organization has it (an alias or a near match doesn't count, and no record is made); the log says `automatic`. Every other link is the person's. |
| Search | The client's directory (up to 5000 people and 5000 organizations) is read with the filters in SQL and ranked in core (`rankEntries`): every word must match a name, email, title, organization, role or website, accents and case aside; a query of 4 or more digits matches phones by digits; whole names rank first. The same ranking serves agents and screens. Relationship, role and organization filters return people only. |
| Tools | `directory.search` and `directory.get` are read tools; `directory.suggest` is internal (`create`). They reach the database through `DirectoryPort` (`packages/agents/src/log/directory-port.ts`) in one client-scoped transaction each. A person's hit carries the contact id that reach out's `to: { contactId }` takes, and never consent details. Notes and names are data in tool results, and the tools' descriptions say so. The built-in skill `directory.lookup` is tool mode over `directory.search`. |
| Suggestions | A suggestion is one inbox item (`kind: review`, `subject_type: directory_suggestion`, assigned to the client admin) with the change, the reason, the sources, the agent and its version in the payload. The run id is kept in the payload, not `run_id`: no run waits for it, and the cron would re-send a resolved item's event to its run. The tool checks the change first (the entries it names are this client's, a new email or number is valid and not someone else's, an organization's name is new) and refuses it otherwise. The same change already waiting gives `duplicate`, not a second item. A test run checks it and makes nothing (`test_run`). Suggestions never carry consent or opt-outs. |
| Accepting | Accepting resolves the inbox item and applies the change in one transaction, through the same contact and organization edits people make, logged with the suggestion, the agent and its version. A change that no longer fits (the address is now someone else's) is refused and the item stays open. A new email or number withdraws that channel's consent, as any edit does (§1.25). The client admin decides, in the inbox or on the directory's Suggestions tab; the operator, who edits the directory fully, may decide too. |
| CSV import | `POST …/directory/import/csv` reads the file with the upload connector's CSV reader and answers what each row would do (add, update the entry it matches, nothing, or can't import) with its changes and problems; nothing is written. `…/import/apply` reads the same file again and applies the chosen rows, each in its own savepoint, with a result per row. Columns are found by common header names (Name or First and Last name, Email, Phone, Company, Title, Relationship, Roles…; for organizations Name, Type, Website, address parts). People match by their id in the tool, then email, then phone; organizations by id, then name. A matched row only fills or changes the fields it has; roles and kinds are added to, never replaced. A person's organization is found by name or added. Phones must be in international form. At most 2000 rows and 1 MB. |
| Zoho CRM and Podio | `…/import/connector` reads through the connection's own read tools, as an agent would (its matrix and the connector's checks apply): Zoho CRM is searched by a word (Contacts for people, Accounts for organizations; its search needs a word), Podio reads one app's items, fields found by their external ids. At most 200 records. They come back as a preview, the suggestions the person applies; applied rows keep their id in `external_refs`, so a later import updates them. No background sync yet. |
| Reach out | Reach out keeps picking people by role bindings and contact ids. Its people picker now shows each person's title and organization and, past 8 people, a search box over the directory's ranking (people already bound stay in view). An agent finds a contact with `directory.search` and passes its id to `comms.reach-out`. |
| Screens | One `DirectoryPanel` for both surfaces: People, Organizations, Import and Suggestions (the last two for the client admin and the operator only), with search and relationship and kind filters. The portal's `/directory` is in the sidebar for every client user; client users read it. The back office shows it as the client page's Directory tab. The inbox card words a suggestion (why, each field, "changes where messages go"). New portal strings are in all six languages. |
| Language (owner, 2026-11-05) | `organization` gains a nullable `language` (one of the six, checked in 0014). The language to write to a person in is core's `languageFor`: the person's own, else their organization's, else the client's default (`tenant.settings.language`), else `en`, with where it came from. Reach out (its plan, fixed templates and composed messages), calls placed and answered, and the messaging services read it through apps/api's `contactLanguage`; human-release codes use it too, with the person's own being their portal choice, else their directory entry's (found by their email), so an organization's language counts. The portal choice wins over the directory entry for release codes (coordinator, 2026-11-05): it is the person's own choice for what they read. `reachLanguage` and `releaseLocale` now call it. Only the language choice changed on those paths. `directory.search` and `directory.get` return each person's `language` and `languageFrom`, and the organization's own; the tools and the `directory.lookup` skill tell the agent to write to each person in it. Screens show "Uses: Spanish (from Acme Corp)" when it is inherited, with a language field on people and organizations. A suggested language change (a person's or an organization's) is flagged like an address change and applies only on acceptance. |
| Contact languages | Contacts used to accept any `xx` or `xx-YY` tag; new values must be one of the six (Zod, `invalid_request` otherwise). Values saved before are kept: `supportedLanguage` reads a regional tag as its language (`es-MX` → `es`, `pt` → `pt-BR`) and skips anything else (`ja`), so the organization's or the client's applies. The contact editor sends the language only when it changed, so an old value isn't refused on an unrelated save. |
| Not built | A background sync from Zoho CRM, Podio or QuickBooks (M9); merging duplicate entries; deleting entries; a change history screen (the logs are kept); the supervisor's directory upkeep (§1.28) can call `directory.suggest` once it lands (W4-B: built, next row). |
| Directory upkeep (W4-B, 2026-11-06) | The supervisor's daily check (`supervisor.daily-check`, in v1 and Sloan's v2 alike, so no template changes) ends by suggesting, through `directory.suggest`'s own port, each vendor on the client's transactions of the last 30 days that the directory lacks (no organization linked to it or of its name, case aside) and that was never suggested before, waiting, accepted or declined (a declined one isn't asked again): `add_organization` of kind vendor, with the transactions as sources, at most 5 a day, newest first. Deterministic, no model; accepting it links the vendor as any new vendor organization does. A test run's check makes none (`test_run`); a failure is logged and never hides the findings. Likely duplicates and people seen in documents (a sender's email) are not looked for yet. The `DirectoryPort` gains an optional `unlistedVendors` (one grouped query, the exclusions in its WHERE). // DECISION |

### 1.28 The supervisor (owner, 2026-11-05; design)

A platform template, `supervisor`, seeded like the Coordinator and forked into each client by the operator (onboarding offers it). It keeps watch over the client's account and reports; it never changes agents, permissions, connections or runs.

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Triggers | New events: `run.failed` (with the agent, the failing step and its error), `connection.needs_attention` (expired or revoked sign-in, failed sync, a website login stopped, a code-service alert). The supervisor's workflows run on those events and on a daily schedule (08:00 in the client's timezone) for a health check. |
| Reading the account | Platform tools, read-only, over the client's own data: `health.runs` (recent and failed runs, the failing step and error), `health.connections` (status, stops, expiries), `health.inbox` (approvals, codes and requests waiting on people, and for how long), `health.agents` (active or paused, live version, last run, last test). |
| Asking agents | `agents.ask(agentId, question)`: runs one of the client's other agents once on the question, as a test run does (never writes, no external action, no release asked), within a small budget, and returns its answer as a tool result. Only agents of the same client. |
| Reporting | `supervisor.report` (internal): a finding with a fingerprint (the same open issue is never reported twice; a reminder after a set time). To the client admin: an inbox item in plain steps ("QuickBooks needs you to sign in again: Connections, QuickBooks, Sign in"), client-safe (invariant 11: no bundles, prompts, instructions or cost). To the operator: an operator inbox item with the details (agent, version, step, error). The daily check writes a short health summary shown on the portal's overview. |
| Directory upkeep | Once §1.27 is in: suggests directory additions it notices (a sender or vendor seen in documents that isn't in the directory, likely duplicates), through `directory.suggest` only. |
| Limits | Mode `suggest_only`; internal writes only (findings, summaries, suggestions); it can't retry runs, change settings or loosen anything; budgets as any agent. Talking with it in the portal waits for threads (`packages/threads`). |

Decisions made while building the supervisor (2026-11-05; built without a migration):

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Events | `run.failed` and `connection.needs_attention` (and `health.checked`, the daily summary) are platform event types; their payloads are Zod schemas in core (`supervisor/health.ts`), used by senses to check every emit. `run.failed` is emitted in the transaction that marks the run failed (`executeRun`'s finish), live runs only: a test run emits nothing, as before. `connection.needs_attention` is emitted where the trouble is recorded: a website login's stop (`login_stopped`), each new code-service alert (`code_alert`, with no connection: an alert belongs to an agent identity), and a connector's `ReconnectRequiredError` (`signin_expired`), which now also marks the connection `error` with a `connection_change` row, once, through a runtime port. `sync_failed` is in the schema for the QuickBooks and Gmail syncs (M9, M10); nothing emits it yet. |
| No loops | The trigger router never starts an agent on a `run.failed` about its own run (a rule for every agent, not a filter the supervisor must remember). The supervisor reports its own failures to the operator only. Findings are inbox items, not events, so reporting starts nothing. Asks are test runs, which emit nothing. |
| Health tools | `health.runs`, `health.connections`, `health.inbox`, `health.agents`: platform tools (`sideEffect: none`) over a `HealthPort`, one `withTenant` transaction each. Every output has `sources`. Token expiry dates stay in the vault (credentials are write-only, invariant 13): `health.connections` reports when the platform last found a connection needing a person and why (from `connection_change`) instead. |
| `agents.ask` | A platform tool of its own, not the Coordinator's `agents.route-ask`: the supervisor asks any agent of its client, while `route-ask` is about routing a person's ask in a thread and enforcing `canCommand` and the thread's step budget, which waits for threads. Route-ask (and `agents.review`, also referenced by the Coordinator and not built) can later be skills over this tool. The ask runs the asked agent's live version as its own test run (`workflow_slug` `agents.ask`, the asker in its trigger), in the asker's client only (looked up inside its `withTenant`; another client's agent, a template, a test copy, a retired agent and the asker itself are refused). The question goes in the task input (data), never in the system prompt; the asked agent is offered only the read tools of its own skills; a tool that writes anything (an internal record included) is refused, a connection needing a release refuses instead of asking, and it can't ask in turn. Budget: 4 model calls and $0.10, or the agent's own if smaller; running out returns "no answer" and opens no item. Its cost is on its own test run. |
| Findings | Inbox items of kind `review`, subject `supervisor_finding`, with the issue's fingerprint in `payload.fingerprint` (ids only: a failed run's agent, workflow and step; a connection and the kind of trouble; a code alert's id). The items a stopped website login and a code alert already open carry the same fingerprint, so the supervisor adds none of its own for those. An issue still open is counted (`occurrences`) and, once `tenant.settings.supervisor.reminderHours` (default 24) has passed since it was opened or last reminded, reminded (`reminders`, `remindedAt`); the portal shows "Reminder". A per-client advisory lock keeps two reports of one issue from both opening an item. Once the client marks theirs done, the issue seen again opens a new one. |
| Client words | The client admin's item is built by the platform, never by the model: a title and plain steps per finding kind, in the client's language (core's `i18n/supervisor.ts`, six languages, using the portal's own labels: Connections, Reconnect, Access). Its payload holds only the fingerprint and the finding's kind, steps and counts. The model's triage note goes only to the operator's item, with the details (agent, version, step, error, connection) and sources. A contract test in apps/api and the agents tests check that the client's item and the portal's responses carry none of it. |
| Daily check | Deterministic (no model): `supervisor.daily-check` reads the four health views, reports failed live runs of the last 24 hours, connections that need a person and client items waiting longer than `tenant.settings.supervisor.waitingHours` (default 48), then writes the summary as a `health.checked` event (counts and sources only; a test run writes none). A failure in that day that a person has already closed is not reported again; a still-open one is counted and reminded when due. The portal's overview shows the latest one and the open findings; the back office's client page shows the same with every finding of both audiences. |
| The template | `supervisor.v1.json`: three workflows (`run-failed` and `connection-needs-attention`: triage with the model, then report; `daily-check` at `0 8 * * *` in the client's timezone), mode `suggest_only`, memory off, budget 8 steps and $0.30. No step can write to a client's tool, so there is no approval to set. The operator adds it from the client page (fork of the template's live version, published, slug `supervisor`, service name "Account check"; a client that has one keeps it) or ticks it at onboarding (on by default); it is test-run and activated like any agent. |
### 1.29 Public site (owner, 2026-11-05)

The public site at `mezonas.com` (`apps/site`), separate from the app.

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Purpose | Tell visitors what Mezonas is, and meet Google's OAuth verification for Gmail: a live homepage on our verified domain that describes the app, with a privacy policy on the same domain linked from it. It also publishes the terms of service and the subprocessors list. |
| Pages | `/` (landing, with "Terms and policies" at the bottom), `/terms`, `/privacy`, `/subprocessors`, and a 404. |
| Build | Plain HTML and CSS: `apps/site/build.mjs` (Node, no dependencies) wraps each page in one layout and copies the design system's CSS, fonts and logos. No framework, no client-side code, no analytics, no cookies. A framework or bundler would add nothing to four pages of text. // DECISION |
| Layering | `apps/site` depends only on `@countersign/design-system` (a dev dependency, its files), has no runtime dependencies, and nothing depends on it (`scripts/check-deps.mjs`). // DECISION |
| Gmail scope | The privacy policy says we ask Google only for `gmail.send` and read no mailbox through Google; mail reaches us by forwarding (§6.5 of ARCHITECTURE). Owner, 2026-11-05: no CASA. A test keeps any other Gmail scope out of the policy. |
| Facts | Every statement in the privacy policy and on the subprocessors page is taken from the code and `docs/operations.md`; what isn't settled is a placeholder. Keep them true when the platform changes (`apps/site/README.md`, "Keeping it true"). |
| Company | Twelve Thirty-Four Consulting LLC, the name on the owner's Twilio compliance profile (EIN not published). Footer: "Mezonas, a product of Twelve Thirty-Four Consulting LLC". (Owner, 2026-11-05.) |
| Text messages | For A2P 10DLC review (Mezonas registers as an ISV; texts go on behalf of each client to its own contacts; use case account notifications and customer care, not marketing): a short `/sms-terms` page, linked from every page's footer, the landing page's "Terms and policies" and the terms, rather than a section in `/terms`, since the terms are the contract with clients and these are for the people they text. The privacy policy gains a "Text messages" section with the carriers' sentences word for word; the terms forbid marketing texts. Every statement matches the code: consent per contact and channel, recorded by the business (or START); STOP and its synonyms opt out of texts, WhatsApp and calls at once; quiet hours. HELP is recognised but nothing replies to it in our code. // DECISION |
| Placeholders | Filled by the owner on 2026-11-05 (`apps/site/README.md`): contact and privacy email, "Dallas, Texas" (no street address), Texas law, deletion within 30 days and backups within a further 30. The AI providers' API terms and the transfer safeguards are written from the providers' published terms, for the lawyer to confirm. No legal page shows "Draft for legal review" now. The legal text is a draft for the owner's lawyer. |
| Deploy | Its own Worker, `mezonas-site` (assets only), on the custom domains `mezonas.com` and `www.mezonas.com`, in a separate PR (`claude/site-deploy`) held until the owner has removed the apex parking records in Cloudflare DNS (`docs/operations.md`, "The public site"). The site must be live before Google verification is submitted. |
| One site, from main | The site is public and the same everywhere, so `deploy.yml`'s own `site` job deploys it on pushes to `main` (and manual runs from `main`), never from a tag, with only the Cloudflare secrets; a failure there never holds up the app's deploy. // DECISION |
| `www` | Serves the same pages with a canonical link to the apex rather than redirecting: a host redirect needs Worker code or a zone rule, and the site has no code. A Redirect Rule (www → apex) can be added in the dashboard at any time. // DECISION |

### 1.30 Decisions made while building M9 (QuickBooks Online, 2026-11-05)

Built against a fake QuickBooks: Intuit's docs were not reachable from the build environment, so
every fact is listed with its status in `packages/connectors/src/qbo/NOTES.md` ("To verify").

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Data (migration 0015, own PR #69) | `cs_runtime` may DELETE `transaction_line`, and only that: a synced transaction's lines are a copy of its lines in the tool now, and itemizing a bill replaces them. Transactions, documents and vendors are still never deleted; every version stays in `raw_record` (insert-only). RLS already covered DELETE. Nothing else needed a table or column: the sync cursor and last sync are on `connection`, the company name in `connection.config.accountName`. // DECISION |
| Routes | The paths registered with the Intuit app: `GET /api/connections/qbo/callback` (the generic forwarder Gmail also returns to, §1.31) forwards Intuit's parameters to the portal page `/connections/qbo/callback`, which finishes the sign-in through `POST /api/connections/qbo/callback` in the client admin's session like every OAuth tool (§1.9); the redirect URI sent to Intuit is the API path. `POST /api/connections/qbo/webhook` (not ARCHITECTURE §14's `/api/webhooks/qbo`) is Intuit's endpoint. `POST /api/ops/connections/:id/sync` (§14) starts a sync now. // DECISION |
| OAuth | Scope `com.intuit.quickbooks.accounting` only. Credentials in the vault (the client's own key, §1.26) hold the tokens, the realm id and the environment; a connection made in sandbox never reaches production. Every refresh stores the new refresh token; refreshes are shared per refresh token in an isolate and their answer reused for a minute, since Intuit rotates refresh tokens and two calls must not spend the same one. Disconnect revokes the refresh token at Intuit (best effort, as §1.9). `QBO_ENV` picks sandbox or production; the redirect URI comes from `APP_ORIGIN`, so there is no `QBO_REDIRECT_URI`. // DECISION |
| Sync | `QboSyncWorkflow`, one instance per sync of one connection, one durable step per page: read the stored cursor, read the next page, download new attachments' files, then store raw records, mapped records, documents and the next cursor in one transaction. Instances are named by connection and time slot (15 minutes for the cron, one minute for webhooks, sign-in and "sync now"), so bursts start one sync. At most 100 pages per instance; the next one goes on from the cursor. // DECISION |
| Initial sync | Vendors and items whole, then 90 days of purchases, bills and vendor credits (by transaction date) and attachments (by last change). History raises no events: no `transaction.created`, and stored documents' `document.received` is kept as already dispatched, so connecting QuickBooks starts no run per old bill. // DECISION |
| CDC | Every 15 minutes (minutes 4, 19, 34 and 49), and on any webhook: all six entities from the cursor, less a minute of overlap. An entity at the 1000-object cap moves the cursor to its latest change rather than to now; past 29 days the sync starts over (raw records dedupe it). Attachments come only from CDC: Intuit sends no webhook for them. Changes after the initial sync raise `transaction.created` or `transaction.updated` (with what changed). // DECISION |
| Webhook | Signature checked (`intuit-signature`, base64 HMAC-SHA256 under `QBO_WEBHOOK_VERIFIER`) before anything is read; both the classic and the CloudEvents body are read; a sync starts for each connected connection to each company named, nothing is processed inline. A company's connections are found by looking in each client in turn (`withTenant`), not through a global index, so no table outside RLS learns which client uses which company. // DECISION |
| Records | Raw records once per (connection, entity, id, SyncToken); a deletion is its own version `deleted`, flagged `is_deleted`, and the transaction is kept with `ext.qbo.deleted`. Transactions are `<Entity>:<Id>`; a Purchase with `Credit: true` is a vendor credit; a purchase's vendor is its payee only when the payee is a Vendor, and its purchaser is the paying account. Lines carry the account's or item's name (what people read); ids are in `raw_record` and the tools. Vendors are linked by `external_refs.qbo`, found by name otherwise, with the company and check names as aliases. Attachments become `document(source='connector')`, stored once per sha256 and linked to their transaction. A record the mappers can't read stays in `raw_record` and holds nothing up. // DECISION |
| Money | QBO sends amounts as JSON numbers: each is read from its shortest decimal text (`Number#toString`), never computed on as a float, and written back as a JSON number straight from its decimal text (`qboJson`). // DECISION |
| Tools | Reads (skills too, §1.9): `qbo.purchase.query`, `qbo.bill.query`, `qbo.vendor.query`, `qbo.item.query`, `qbo.attachable.list`. Writes, each external and behind approval (ARCHITECTURE v0.9): `qbo.bill.update_lines`, `qbo.purchase.update_lines`, `qbo.bill.update_memo`, `qbo.purchase.update_memo`, `qbo.item.update_purchase_cost`, `qbo.attachable.upload`. No create, no delete. Every write reads the record first (the before state), names the SyncToken the agent read, sends QBO's `requestid` derived from the action's idempotency key, and recognises its own earlier write (a retry after a lost answer) by the result rather than by line ids. |
| Itemizing | Refused when proposed (no one is asked) and again before writing if: the total isn't the one the agent read, the record has tax, a line to replace isn't there, or the new lines don't add up to the lines they replace. Item lines must be qty × unit price rounded half up to cents. New lines inherit the replaced line's job, class, billable status and tax code. A total QuickBooks still moved fails the action for a person. // DECISION |
| Attachments | `qbo.attachable.upload` reads one of the call's client's documents through `ConnectorServices.documents` (a document of another client reads as unknown) and is a no-op when the record already has a file of the same name and size. // DECISION |
| Throttle | Per realm in each isolate (450 a minute, 10 at once), plus three retries on 429 (1, 2, 4 s or `Retry-After`) before the step's own retry. A Durable Object per realm is the follow-up if 429s show. // DECISION |
| Portal | QuickBooks appears on Connections like any OAuth tool once offered; the card names the company (`accountName`, a new optional field of the portal connection) rather than the realm id. |
| Bill clerk (starter template, 2026-11-05) | `packages/db/seed/agents/bill-clerk.v1.json`, platform kind, `suggest_only`, the first template that writes to QuickBooks. Workflow `invoice-read` starts on `document.extracted` with `totalsValid` true; publishing a client's copy needs a `qbo` binding (its writes have no connection otherwise). Steps: `docs.match-bill` finds the one synced bill (same vendor by name, alias or forwarding domain; same currency and total to the cent; dated within ± 7 days; document numbers agree, compared on letters and digits, a prefix allowed, a bill without one not ruled out; deleted bills left out); none or several → a person step for the client admin ("We couldn't match invoice X from V to a bill", the reason, the candidates' transaction ids as `sources`), no writes. One → `qbo.bill.query` reads it live and `docs.plan-bill` decides: itemize only when the bill has fewer lines than the invoice, no tax, the invoice's total, and the invoice's lines add up to it exactly; a bill with as many lines → a notice, nothing changed; anything else → a person step with the reason. Itemize → the model (`qbo.map-invoice-lines`, agent mode, reads the invoice and bill only through tools) chooses an item or one of the bill's own accounts per line, nothing else; `docs.bill-lines` builds the lines in code with the invoice's descriptions, quantities, prices and amounts and refuses a missing, repeated or off-bill choice or an item line that isn't qty × price; then three actions, each `approval: required`, one after another: `qbo.bill.update_lines` (all lines replaced, total unchanged), `qbo.attachable.upload` (skipped when the document is the bill's own synced attachment), and, for a bill with no memo, `qbo.bill.update_memo` with "<vendor> invoice <number>, PO <po>" after reading the bill again for its new SyncToken. A notice ends it, citing the document, its reading and the transaction. // DECISION |
| Bill clerk: platform pieces | No migration. `matchBill`, `planBill` and `billLines` in packages/documents (`bill.ts`), as the read tools `docs.match_bill`, `docs.plan_bill`, `docs.bill_lines` and tool-mode skills of the same names (hyphenated); `DocumentsPort.matchBill`. The agent-mode skill `qbo.map-invoice-lines` ships with the connector (`qbo/skills.ts`) and apps/api registers it where qbo is registered. The notify step gains optional `sources` (templates resolving to ids or lists of ids), stored as the message's sources, so a notice can cite its records (invariant 10). The match reads the synced bills, not QuickBooks' query API: the vendor link and our transaction ids live there; a bill entered in QuickBooks reaches us within a minute (webhook) or 15 (CDC), so an invoice uploaded before that asks the client admin. // DECISION |
| Bill clerk: open | An invoice read twice while the first run still waits on approval asks for the same approvals twice (the second writes find the bill done and write nothing; a run after the first finished sees a done bill and proposes nothing); accounts beyond those on the bill need a chart-of-accounts read tool; learned item mappings per client (memory is off); installing the template on production by hand, where the seed never runs; the walk against the sandbox company. |

### 1.31 Decisions made while building M10 (Gmail, send only; 2026-11-05)

Built on `claude/m10-gmail-send`, with no migration: the connection's config and the vault hold
everything. Facts about Google we couldn't check, and the list to verify before a client connects,
are in `packages/connectors/src/gmail/NOTES.md`.

| Topic | Decision |
|---|---|
| Scopes | `gmail.send` and `openid email`, nothing else. `gmail.send` alone can't read which address signed in (the profile needs a read scope), and we need it to show the mailbox on Connections and to send From it; the ID token in Google's token answer carries it. `openid` and `email` are Google's basic sign-in scopes, not Gmail scopes. The privacy policy says so. The owner adds both to the consent screen beside `gmail.send`. // DECISION |
| Sign-in | Authorization code with `access_type=offline`, `prompt=consent` (a refresh token on every consent, reconnects included) and `include_granted_scopes=false`. The callback refuses, storing nothing, a grant without `gmail.send` (Google's granular consent lets a person untick it), a grant with any other scope, an unverified address, and an ID token for another client id. The refusal is 422 `sign_in_refused` with plain words (`SignInRefusedError`, new in the connector framework). The ID token's signature isn't checked: it comes straight from Google's token endpoint over TLS. // DECISION |
| Redirect | Google's client registers `<origin>/api/connections/gmail/callback` per environment (owner, 2026-11-05), so Gmail returns to that API path, `GOOGLE_REDIRECT_URI` when set, else built from `APP_ORIGIN`. `GET /api/connections/:connector/callback` only forwards the query to the portal's `/connections/:connector/callback` page (no store, no referrer), which posts it to the existing callback route in the person's session, as for Zoho and Podio. No `wrangler.jsonc` change: the default matches every registered URI. // DECISION |
| Who connects | The client admin, or the person whose mailbox it is: a client user may connect Gmail (only Gmail, `PERSONAL_CONNECTORS` in apps/api) when no one else's Gmail is connected, and disconnect it only if they connected it. The portal shows Connect and Disconnect to them accordingly (`personal` and `connectedByYou` on the portal's connection). The operator never connects. One Gmail connection per client (v1's rule), so a client connects one mailbox: an agent's own, a shared one or a person's. // DECISION |
| Tokens | In the vault (the client's key), with the address and the granted scopes; `connection.config` holds only `{address, scopes: ["gmail.send"]}`. Refresh before expiry and once after a 401; `invalid_grant` marks the connection as needing a person (the supervisor's `connection.needs_attention`). Disconnect revokes the refresh token at Google (the whole grant), then drops the credentials; an already revoked token is not an error. // DECISION |
| `gmail.send` | External, verb `create`, behind approval by default, with the same input as `email.send` (`to`, `subject`, `text`, `replyTag`), so reach out builds one input for either email connection. Its before state is the email as it would go: contact, to, from, reply-to, subject and text. The contact checks (a contact of this client, email consent, not opted out) run in code before and again at the send, through the same `EmailServices` as Resend. Output adds Gmail's `threadId`. // DECISION |
| Sent once | Gmail takes no idempotency key and `gmail.send` can't list sent mail. So the send is recorded as started (`raw_record` `EmailAttempt`, one per key) before Gmail is called; a re-run returns the recorded email, and a started send with no record is never sent again: the action fails as unconfirmed and the operator gets an item to check the mailbox. `EmailServices` gains optional `startAttempt`, `attemptFor` and `replyAddress`; `gmail.send` refuses to run without them. // DECISION |
| The message | Built in code (`gmail/mime.ts`): `From` the connected address, `To`, `Reply-To` the client's forwarding address with `+<reach-out id>` (none while the client has no forwarding address), the subject as RFC 2047 encoded words when it isn't plain ASCII (at most 75 characters each, never splitting a character), `Date`, our own `Message-ID`, `text/plain; charset=UTF-8` in base64 with CRLF line endings. Header values can't carry line breaks; addresses and the Message-ID are checked. `raw` is URL-safe base64 with padding. // DECISION |
| Replies | They come back to the client's forwarding address through the tagged Reply-To and are matched as for Resend (§1.25): the tag, the client's stored address and the contact's own address. The reply's own words are cut at the quoted history, now also at Gmail's line above the quote in the portal's six languages ("El … escribió:", "Le … a écrit :", "Em … escreveu:", "Il … ha scritto:", "Am … schrieb …:"). // DECISION |
| Reach out | `CHANNEL_TOOLS.gmail = {email: "gmail.send"}`: the operator binds an agent's email channel to the client's Gmail connection like Resend's; Gmail carries email only. Language, approval of the plan, one external action per attempt, grants and human release are reach out's, unchanged: an agent whose grant turns `create` off, or a connection with human release on and no live release, sends nothing (`not_allowed` with the reason). Test runs send nothing. // DECISION |
| Portal | The Connections card says "Send email from your Gmail. We can't read your mailbox." in the reader's language (six languages; the English is the manifest's description). |
| Test email | "Send a test email" on a connected Gmail (2026-11-05; for clients checking the connection, and for Google's demo video of `gmail.send`): `POST /api/connections/gmail/test-send` in the portal, for the client admin and the person who connected the mailbox (`mayManage`'s rule), and `POST /api/ops/clients/:id/connections/gmail/test-send` in the back office for the Sandbox client only (403 `not_sandbox` elsewhere). Both bodies are strictly empty: the email goes from the connection's stored address to that same address, never anywhere else, and is refused (409 `no_address`) without one or when the tokens' address differs. Plain text in the reader's language (core's `GMAIL_TEST_WORDS`; English in the back office).
